### Lưu đồ

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá tình trạng huyết áp của bệnh nhân.
- Nếu huyết áp bình thường: Tiếp tục theo dõi.
- Nếu huyết áp cao: Tiến hành các bước tiếp theo.
2. **Phân loại huyết áp**:
- Huyết áp cao (130-139/80-89): Khuyến nghị thay đổi lối sống.
- Huyết áp cao hơn (≥140/90): Tiến hành điều trị.
3. **Điều trị**:
- Bắt đầu điều trị bằng thuốc: Chọn loại thuốc phù hợp.
- Theo dõi hiệu quả điều trị: Đánh giá sau 1-2 tháng.
4. **Đánh giá lại**:
- Nếu huyết áp vẫn cao: Điều chỉnh liều hoặc thay đổi thuốc.
- Nếu huyết áp đạt mục tiêu: Tiếp tục theo dõi định kỳ.
5. **Kết thúc**: Đảm bảo bệnh nhân hiểu rõ về tình trạng và kế hoạch điều trị.
[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Assess blood pressure levels.
- Classify blood pressure:
- Normal: &#x3C;120/&#x3C;80 mm Hg
- Elevated: 120-129/&#x3C;80 mm Hg
- Stage 1 Hypertension: 130-139/80-89 mm Hg
- Stage 2 Hypertension: ≥140/≥90 mm Hg
2. **For all adults with average BP ≥140/90 mm Hg:**
- Initiate medication therapy in addition to lifestyle changes.
3. **For selected adults with average BP ≥130/80 mm Hg:**
- Consider medication if they have:
- Clinical cardiovascular disease
- Previous stroke
- Diabetes
- Chronic kidney disease
- Increased 10-year cardiovascular risk (≥7.5%)
4. **For adults with average BP ≥130/80 mm Hg and lower risk (&#x3C;7.5%):**
- Initiate medication if BP remains ≥130/80 mm Hg after 3-6 months of lifestyle modification.
5. **For all adults with stage 2 hypertension:**
- Initiate antihypertensive drug therapy with 2 first-line agents in a single-pill combination.
6. **Home blood pressure monitoring:**
- Use standardized measurement and treatment protocols.
- Avoid reliance on cuffless devices until proven reliable.
7. **For severe hypertension (>180/120 mm Hg) without acute organ damage:**
- Evaluate and treat in outpatient setting with timely initiation or intensification of oral antihypertensive medications.
8. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
2. **Assess Blood Pressure**
- If BP is high, proceed to step 3.
- If BP is normal, provide lifestyle recommendations and monitor.
3. **Determine Type of Hypertension**
- If resistant hypertension, screen for primary aldosteronism.
- If not resistant, proceed to step 4.
4. **Evaluate for Secondary Causes**
- If secondary cause identified, treat accordingly.
- If no secondary cause, proceed to step 5.
5. **Lifestyle and Psychosocial Approaches**
- Recommend potassium-based salt substitutes if appropriate.
6. **Initiate Pharmacotherapy**
- If CVD risk ≥10%, initiate BP-lowering medications.
- If CVD risk &#x3C;10%, monitor and consider lifestyle interventions.
7. **Monitor and Adjust Treatment**
- Regularly assess BP and adjust medications as needed.
8. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Acute Intracerebral Hemorrhage**
- If SBP >220 mm Hg
- Use continuous intravenous drug infusion and close BP monitoring to lower SBP.
- If acute spontaneous ICH requiring acute BP lowering
- Careful titration to ensure smooth, nonlabile, and sustained control of BP.

2. **Acute Ischemic Stroke**
- N/A
- If undergoing successful brain reperfusion with endovascular treatment for a large vessel occlusion
- Avoid lowering SBP &#x3C;140 mm Hg within the first 24 to 72 hours after reperfusion.

3. **Mild Cognitive Impairment and Dementia**
- If adults with hypertension
- BP lowering is reasonable to prevent cognitive decline and dementia.
- Goal of &#x3C;130 mm Hg SBP recommended to prevent mild cognitive impairment and dementia.

4. **Hypertension and Pregnancy**
- If SBP ≥160 mm Hg or DBP ≥110 mm Hg confirmed on repeat measurement
- Administer antihypertensive medication to lower BP to &#x3C;160/&#x3C;110 mm Hg within 30 to 60 minutes.
- If chronic hypertension
- Administer antihypertensive therapy to achieve BP &#x3C;140/90 mm Hg.
- Counseling on low-dose aspirin for preeclampsia risk reduction.

5. **Resistant Hypertension and Renal Denervation**
- Evaluate for secondary causes and review medications.
- Multidisciplinary team evaluation for RDN.
- Discuss benefits and risks of RDN compared to continuing medical therapy.

6. **Hypertensive Emergencies**
- For severe hypertension (>180/120 mm Hg) without acute target organ damage
- Intermittent use of additional antihypertensive medications is not recommended.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

| Section                                      | Content                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
|----------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Authors**                                  | Jones et al                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| **Title**                                    | 2025 High Blood Pressure Guideline                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| **Preamble**                                 | Since 1980, the American College of Cardiology (ACC) and American Heart Association (AHA) have translated scientific evidence into clinical practice guidelines with recommendations to improve cardiovascular health. These guidelines provide a foundation for the delivery of quality cardiovascular care. The ACC and AHA sponsor the development and publication of clinical practice guidelines without commercial support. |
| **Intended Use**                            | Clinical practice guidelines provide recommendations applicable to patients with or at risk of developing cardiovascular disease (CVD). The focus is on medical practice in the United States, but these guidelines are relevant to patients throughout the world. Guidelines are intended to define practices meeting the needs of patients in most, but not all, circumstances and should not replace clinical judgment. |
| **Clinical Implementation**                  | Management in accordance with guideline recommendations is effective only when followed by both practitioners and patients. Adherence to recommendations can be enhanced by shared decision-making between clinicians and patients, with patient engagement in selecting interventions based on individual values, preferences, associated conditions, and comorbidities. |
| **Methodology and Modernization**            | The ACC/AHA Joint Committee on Clinical Practice Guidelines continuously reviews, updates, and modifies guideline methodology based on published standards and internal reevaluation. Guidelines are written and presented in a modular recommendation format to enhance user-friendliness. An independent evidence review committee is commissioned when there are ≥1 questions deemed of importance. |
| **Relationships With Industry and Other Entities** | The ACC and AHA have rigorous policies and methods to ensure that documents are developed without bias or improper influence. The complete policy on relationships with industry and other entities (RWI) can be found online. Appendix 1 of the guideline lists writing committee members’ comprehensive and relevant RWI. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Begin the process of developing clinical practice guidelines.

2. **Review Scientific Evidence**
- The ACC and AHA review scientific evidence and classify it using systematic methods.

3. **Develop Recommendations**
- Create recommendations based on the reviewed evidence.
- **Decision Point:** Is the evidence sufficient?
- **Yes:** Proceed to the next step.
- **No:** Reassess the evidence.

4. **Engage Writing Committee**
- Form a writing committee with diverse expertise and backgrounds.
- Ensure representation across geographic regions, sexes, races, and clinical practice settings.

5. **Draft Guidelines**
- Draft the guidelines in a modular format for user-friendliness.
- Include tables of recommendations and brief synopses.

6. **Peer Review**
- Submit the draft for peer review to ensure accuracy and relevance.

7. **Publish Guidelines**
- Publish the guidelines and make them available to healthcare professionals.

8. **Ongoing Review**
- Continuously review and update guidelines based on new evidence and feedback.
- **Decision Point:** Is there new evidence?
- **Yes:** Update recommendations.
- **No:** Maintain current guidelines.

9. **End**
- Conclude the guideline development process.
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Markdown Table

| Section | Content |
|---------|---------|
| **Title** | 2025 High Blood Pressure Guideline |
| **Authors** | Jones et al |
| **Clinical Statements** | It is of utmost clinical importance that merit formal systematic review to determine which patients are most likely to benefit from a drug, device, or treatment strategy, and to what degree. |
| **Writing Committee Organization** | The writing committee consisted of general/preventive cardiologists, interventional cardiologists, cardiac imaging experts, nephrologists, internists, a neurologist, a gerontologist, cardiovascular epidemiologists, advanced practice nurses, a clinical pharmacist, a physician associate, and a patient advocate. |
| **Affiliations** | The writing committee included representatives from AHA, ACC, ABC, AAPA, ACCP, SGIM, PCNA, AMA, AANP, NMA, ACPM, ASPC, and AGS. |
| **Guideline-Directed Medical Therapy (GDMT)** | The term GDMT encompasses clinical evaluation, diagnostic testing, and both pharmacological and procedural treatments. |
| **Recommendations** | Recommendations are limited to drugs, devices, and treatments approved for clinical use in the United States. |
| **Disclosure** | On February 28, 2024, a writing committee member disclosed relationships with Amgen and Novartis, which were deemed relevant to the guideline. |
| **Approval Process** | The document was approved for publication by the governing bodies of the ACC and the AHA and was endorsed by various organizations. |
| **Methodology and Evidence Review** | The recommendations are evidence-based, derived from literature involving human subjects, published in English, and indexed in relevant databases. |
| **Scope of the Guideline** | This guideline is intended to be a resource for clinical and public health professionals and replaces the previously published guideline for the management of high blood pressure in adults. |
| **Exclusions** | This guideline does not provide recommendations on blood pressure management in patient populations with chronic coronary disease, heart failure, atrial fibrillation, valvular heart disease, and aortic disease. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Begin the guideline development process.
2. **Evidence Review**
- Conduct an extensive evidence review.
- Include literature from human subjects, published in English, indexed in MEDLINE, EMBASE, Cochrane Library, etc.
3. **Criteria for Evidence Review**
- Check for absence of current authoritative systematic review.
- Assess feasibility of defining benefit and risk.
- Ensure relevance to a substantial number of patients.
- Evaluate likelihood of actionable recommendations.
4. **Writing Committee Formation**
- Assemble a diverse writing committee with representatives from various organizations.
5. **Draft Recommendations**
- Develop recommendations based on systematic review findings.
- Mark recommendations as “SR” if based on systematic review.
6. **Peer Review Process**
- Appoint a peer review committee to review the document.
- Distribute reviewers’ RWI information to the writing committee.
7. **Approval for Publication**
- Submit the document for approval by governing bodies of ACC and AHA.
- Endorsement by collaborating organizations.
8. **Finalization**
- Publish the guideline.
9. **End**
- Conclude the guideline development process.
[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Initiation of Pharmacological BP Treatment**
- Assess overall cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk.
- Determine blood pressure (BP) thresholds for treatment initiation.
- Consider lifestyle modifications as first-line therapy.
- If BP remains elevated, proceed to pharmacological treatment.

2. **Risk Assessment**
- Use pooled cohort equations (PCEs) to estimate risk for atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD).
- Compare with PREVENT equations for total cardiovascular disease risk.
- Evaluate diverse racial and ethnic groups in risk assessment.

3. **Treatment Options**
- Lifestyle modifications (diet, exercise).
- Antihypertensive medications based on individual risk factors.
- Regular monitoring and follow-up to assess treatment efficacy.

4. **Follow-Up**
- Reassess BP and overall CVD risk periodically.
- Adjust treatment plan as necessary based on follow-up results.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Assess Patient**: Determine if the patient has hypertension without clinical CVD.
- If yes, proceed to step 2.
- If no, consider alternative guidelines.

2. **Estimate 10-Year Risk for CVD**: Use PREVENT model for adults aged 30-79.
- Incorporate kidney function measures and social risk factors.
- If risk is high, proceed to step 3.
- If risk is low, monitor and reassess.

3. **Determine BP Threshold for Treatment**: Refer to Section 5.2.1.
- If BP is above threshold, initiate pharmacological treatment.
- If BP is below threshold, continue lifestyle modifications.

4. **Set BP Goal for Treatment**: Refer to Section 5.2.7.
- If BP goal is achieved, continue monitoring.
- If BP goal is not achieved, reassess treatment plan.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| CLASS (STRENGTH) OF RECOMMENDATION | LEVEL (QUALITY) OF EVIDENCE |
|-------------------------------------|------------------------------|
| Class 1 (STRONG)                    | Level A                      |
| Suggested phrases for writing recommendations: |                              |
| Is recommended                       | High-quality evidence from more than 1 RCT |
| Is indicated/useful/effective/beneficial | Meta-analyses of high-quality RCTs |
| Should be performed/administered/other | One or more RCTs corroborated by high-quality registry studies |
| Comparative-Effectiveness Phrases:   |                              |
| Treatment/strategy A is recommended/indicated in preference to treatment B | Level B-R (Randomized) |
| Treatment A should be chosen over treatment B | Moderate-quality evidence from 1 or more RCTs |
|                                     | Meta-analyses of moderate-quality RCTs |
|                                     | Level B-NR (Nonrandomized)  |
| Class 2a (MODERATE)                 | Benefit >> Risk              |
| Suggested phrases for writing recommendations: |                              |
| Is reasonable                        | Moderate-quality evidence from 1 or more well-designed, well-executed nonrandomized studies, observational studies, or registry studies |
| Can be useful/effective/beneficial   | Meta-analyses of such studies |
| Comparative-Effectiveness Phrases:   |                              |
| Treatment/strategy A is probably recommended/indicated in preference to treatment B | Level C-LD (Limited Data) |
| It is reasonable to choose treatment A over treatment B | Randomized or nonrandomized observational or registry studies with limitations of design or execution |
|                                     | Meta-analyses of such studies |
|                                     | Physiological or mechanistic studies in human subjects |
| Class 2b (WEAK)                     | Benefit ≥ Risk               |
| Suggested phrases for writing recommendations: |                              |
| May/might be reasonable              | Level C-EO (Expert Opinion)  |
| May/might be considered              | Consensus of expert opinion based on clinical experience |
| Usefulness/effectiveness is unknown/unclear/uncertain or not well-established | COR and LOE are determined independently (any COR may be paired with any LOE). |
|                                     | A recommendation with LOE C does not imply that the recommendation is weak. Many important clinical questions addressed in guidelines do not lend themselves to clinical trials. Although RCTs are unavailable, there may be a very clear clinical consensus that a particular test or therapy is useful or effective. |
| Class 3: No Benefit (MODERATE)      | Benefit = Risk               |
| Suggested phrases for writing recommendations: |                              |
| Is not recommended                   | (Generally, LOE A or B use only) |
| Is not indicated/useful/effective/beneficial | The outcome or result of the intervention should be specified (an improved clinical outcome or increased diagnostic accuracy or incremental prognostic information). |
| Should not be performed/administered/other | For comparative-effectiveness recommendations (COR 1 and 2a; LOE A and B only), studies that support the use of comparator verbs should involve direct comparisons of the treatments or strategies being evaluated. |
| Class 3: HARM (STRONG)              | Risk > Benefit               |
| Suggested phrases for writing recommendations: |                              |
| Potentially harmful                  | Suggested phrases for writing recommendations: |
| Causes harm                          | Should not be performed/administered/other |
| Associated with excess morbidity/mortality | COR indicates Class of Recommendation; EO, expert opinion; LD, limited data; LOE, Level of Evidence; NR, nonrandomized; R, randomized; and RCT, randomized controlled trial. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Start
2. Assess Patient Condition
- If condition is severe, proceed to Class 3: HARM
- If condition is moderate, proceed to Class 2a or 2b
- If condition is mild, proceed to Class 1
3. Class 1: Strong Recommendation
- Is recommended
- Is indicated/useful/effective/beneficial
- Should be performed/administered
- Comparative-Effectiveness Phrases
4. Class 2a: Moderate Recommendation
- Is reasonable
- Can be useful/effective/beneficial
- Comparative-Effectiveness Phrases
5. Class 2b: Weak Recommendation
- May/might be reasonable
- May/might be considered
- Usefulness/effectiveness is unknown/unclear/uncertain
6. Class 3: No Benefit
- Is not recommended
- Is not indicated/useful/effective/beneficial
- Should not be performed/administered
7. Class 3: HARM
- Potentially harmful
- Causes harm
- Should not be performed/administered
8. End
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**: Patient presents with high blood pressure (BP).
- Decision Point: Is BP ≥ 130/80 mm Hg?
- Yes: Proceed to Step 2.
- No: Monitor and reassess.

2. **Categorize BP**:
- Decision Point: What is the BP category?
- Normal: BP &#x3C; 120/80 mm Hg.
- Elevated: BP 120-129/&#x3C;80 mm Hg.
- Stage 1 Hypertension: BP 130-139/80-89 mm Hg.
- Stage 2 Hypertension: BP ≥ 140/90 mm Hg.

3. **Management Recommendations**:
- For Normal: Encourage lifestyle modifications.
- For Elevated: Recommend lifestyle changes and monitor.
- For Stage 1: Consider lifestyle changes and assess CVD risk.
- For Stage 2: Initiate antihypertensive therapy and lifestyle changes.

4. **Follow-Up**:
- Decision Point: Is BP controlled after intervention?
- Yes: Continue monitoring.
- No: Reassess treatment plan and consider referral to specialist.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Sơ đồ/Lưu đồ

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá tình trạng huyết áp
- Nếu huyết áp > 140/90 mm Hg
- Tiến hành kiểm tra các yếu tố nguy cơ
- Nếu có yếu tố nguy cơ (như tiểu đường, béo phì, CKD)
- Khuyến nghị điều trị
- Nếu không có yếu tố nguy cơ
- Theo dõi huyết áp định kỳ
- Nếu huyết áp ≤ 140/90 mm Hg
- Đánh giá tình trạng kiểm soát huyết áp
- Nếu kiểm soát không đạt (&#x3C;130/80 mm Hg)
- Khuyến nghị thay đổi lối sống và theo dõi
- Nếu kiểm soát đạt
- Tiếp tục theo dõi định kỳ
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá bệnh nhân
- **Bước 1**: Đo huyết áp tại văn phòng
- **Khuyến nghị 1**: Sử dụng phương pháp tiêu chuẩn hóa để đo huyết áp
- **Khuyến nghị 2**: Sử dụng phương pháp dao động với thiết bị tự động

- **Bước 2**: Đánh giá nguy cơ CVD

- **Bước 3**: Xem xét điều trị
- Nếu SBP/DBP &#x3C; 120/80 mm Hg
- **Kết luận**: Nguy cơ CVD vẫn cao
- Nếu không
- **Kết luận**: Tiếp tục theo dõi và điều trị
- Nếu huyết áp &#x3C; 120/80 mm Hg
- **Kết luận**: Tiếp tục theo dõi

### Bảng biểu

| STT | Hướng dẫn đo huyết áp tại văn phòng                                                                                     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | Bệnh nhân nên tránh caffeine, tập thể dục và hút thuốc ít nhất 30 phút trước khi đo. Đảm bảo bệnh nhân đã đi tiểu.   |
| 2   | Sử dụng thiết bị đo huyết áp đã được xác thực về độ chính xác (validatebp.org).                                       |
| 3   | Sử dụng kích thước băng quấn đúng trên cánh tay trần.                                                                  |
| 4   | Cánh tay của bệnh nhân nên được hỗ trợ ở mức tim.                                                                      |
| 5   | Để bệnh nhân thư giãn, ngồi trên ghế (chân để trên sàn, chân không bắt chéo, và lưng được hỗ trợ) trong hơn 5 phút. |
| 6   | Cả bệnh nhân và bác sĩ không nên nói chuyện trong thời gian nghỉ hoặc trong khi đo. Bệnh nhân không nên sử dụng điện thoại. |
| 7   | Đo huyết áp nên được thực hiện trong phòng có nhiệt độ được kiểm soát.                                                |
| 8   | Thực hiện 2 hoặc nhiều lần đo huyết áp cách nhau ít nhất 1 phút. Tính trung bình các chỉ số và cung cấp cho bệnh nhân. |

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Bệnh nhân đến văn phòng.
2. **Chuẩn bị bệnh nhân**:
- Tránh caffeine, tập thể dục, và hút thuốc ít nhất 30 phút trước.
- Đảm bảo bệnh nhân đã đi tiểu.
3. **Thiết bị đo**:
- Sử dụng thiết bị đã được xác thực (validatebp.org).
- Sử dụng kích thước băng quấn đúng.
4. **Vị trí bệnh nhân**:
- Cánh tay hỗ trợ ở mức tim.
- Bệnh nhân thư giãn trong hơn 5 phút.
5. **Thời gian nghỉ**:
- Không nói chuyện hoặc sử dụng điện thoại.
6. **Điều kiện môi trường**:
- Đo trong phòng có nhiệt độ kiểm soát.
7. **Thực hiện đo**:
- Thực hiện 2 hoặc nhiều lần đo cách nhau ít nhất 1 phút.
8. **Tính trung bình**:
- Tính trung bình các chỉ số đo và cung cấp cho bệnh nhân.
9. **Kết thúc**: Cung cấp kết quả cho bệnh nhân.
[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
| Diagnostic Tests                                                                                     |
|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Complete blood count                                                                                 |
| Serum sodium, potassium, calcium                                                                      |
| Serum creatinine with estimation of glomerular filtration rate (based on the 2021 CKD-EPI Creatinine Equation) |
| Lipid profile                                                                                         |
| Fasting blood glucose or Hemoglobin A1c                                                              |
| Thyroid-stimulating hormone                                                                            |
| Urinalysis                                                                                           |
| Urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio; urine protein-to-creatinine ratio                                 |
| ECG                                                                                                  |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Patient Evaluation
- Comprehensive medical history
- Physical examination
- Laboratory tests (complete blood count, serum electrolytes, serum creatinine, lipid profile, glucose or hemoglobin A1c, thyroid-stimulating hormone, urinalysis, urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio)
- Diagnostic procedures (12-lead ECG)
- Purpose: Establish baseline CVD risk and inform management decisions

2. If hypertension is suspected or confirmed
- Perform laboratory tests and diagnostic procedures
- Repeat pertinent laboratory tests at least annually

3. Monitor for:
- Potential adverse effects of therapies (e.g., serum electrolytes)
- Development or progression of kidney disease (e.g., urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio, creatinine-based or cystatin-C based estimated glomerular filtration rate)
- Changes in predicted CVD risk (e.g., lipid profile)

4. Evaluate for:
- Subclinical CVD (e.g., left ventricular hypertrophy)
- Cardiac biomarkers, echocardiography, and coronary artery calcium scoring for refined risk estimation

5. If secondary causes of hypertension are suspected
- Consider additional diagnostic evaluation
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Markdown Table

| COR | LOE | Recommendations                                                                                                           |
|-----|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | A   | 1. In adults with suspected hypertension, out-of-office BP measurements by either ABPM or HBPM are recommended to confirm the diagnosis of hypertension.1,2 |
| 1   | A   | 2. In adults who are taking antihypertensive medication, HBPM is recommended for monitoring the titration of BP-lowering medication, along with cointerventions such as patient education, telehealth counseling, and clinical interventions.2–6 |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Begin the process of BP measurement.
2. **Measurement Method**
- Choose between ABPM or HBPM.
- If ABPM:
- Wear a fully automated device for 24 hours.
- Collect BP readings at 15-30 minute intervals during the day and 30-60 minute intervals at night.
- Analyze data for mean BP, daytime/nighttime ratios, early-morning surge, variability, and hypotension recognition.
- If HBPM:
- Measure BP at home using an oscillometric device over days to weeks.
- Analyze data for mean BP, daytime/evening variability, and variability across days.
3. **Data Interpretation**
- Compare ABPM and HBPM results with office BP readings.
- Use thresholds provided in Table 7 for interpretation.
4. **Diagnosis Confirmation**
- Confirm diagnosis of hypertension based on out-of-office BP measurements.
5. **Management Plan**
- For patients on antihypertensive medication, monitor titration using HBPM.
- Implement cointerventions (patient education, telehealth, etc.) for better BP control.
6. **Outcome Assessment**
- Evaluate BP control rates and adjust management as necessary.
7. **End**
- Conclude the BP management process.
[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
| CLINICAL STATEMENTS AND GUIDELINES | Home Blood Pressure Monitoring |
|-------------------------------------|-------------------------------|
| Device and blood pressure cuff      | Use a blood pressure device that has been validated for accuracy. Check with your clinician or other members of your care team, and the following website for devices: www.validatebp.org. Use the correct cuff size matched to the size of your arm. |
| Patient preparation                 | Avoid smoking, caffeinated beverages, or exercise within 30 minutes before blood pressure measurements. |
| Positioning of patient and cuff     | Place the cuff on a bare arm, and your arm should be supported at heart level. The bottom of the cuff should be placed directly above the bend of the elbow. You should relax, and sit in a chair (feet on floor, legs uncrossed, and back supported) for at least 5 minutes. |
| Blood pressure measurement           | While relaxing and measuring your blood pressure, please do not talk, use your phone, or watch TV. You should take 2 readings 1 min apart twice a day (for a total of 4 readings): 2 readings in the morning after emptying your bladder (urinating) and before taking your medication and eating; and 2 readings at bedtime before sleep. Check blood pressure for 3 days (minimum) to 7 days (preferred) before your appointment or interaction with your clinician. Document your daily blood pressure measurements in writing or electronically. Share your readings with the clinician taking care of you. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Begin the home blood pressure monitoring process.

2. **Device Selection**
- Use a validated blood pressure device.
- Check with clinician or care team.
- Visit www.validatebp.org for device validation.

3. **Cuff Size**
- Ensure correct cuff size is used.

4. **Patient Preparation**
- Avoid smoking, caffeine, or exercise 30 minutes prior to measurement.

5. **Positioning**
- Place cuff on bare arm.
- Support arm at heart level.
- Relax in a chair for at least 5 minutes.

6. **Measurement Process**
- Do not talk, use phone, or watch TV during measurement.
- Take 2 readings 1 minute apart, twice a day (total of 4 readings).
- Morning: After urinating and before medication/food.
- Evening: Before sleep.

7. **Duration of Monitoring**
- Check blood pressure for 3 to 7 days before clinician appointment.

8. **Documentation**
- Document daily blood pressure readings.
- Share readings with clinician.

9. **End**
- Complete the home blood pressure monitoring process.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

|--------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------|
| Higher sodium intake                       | Genetic variants                           |
| Lower potassium intake                     | Overweight/obesity                         |
| Lower calcium/magnesium intake             | Lower physical activity/fitness            |
| Lower diet quality (lower intake of fruits/vegetables, plant proteins, fiber) | Sleep disturbances (related to duration, quality, regularity, and/or disordered breathing) |
| Alcohol intake                             | Psychosocial stressors                     |
|                                            | Air pollution                              |

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Patient Diagnosis**
- Causes of Hypertension
- Elevated BP and hypertension reflect a complex interplay of behavioral, environmental, hormonal, and genetic influences across the lifespan.
- Diet quality is significantly associated with elevated BP and its sequelae.
- Higher sodium intake
- Lower potassium intake
- Alcohol overuse
- Low intake of fiber, calcium, magnesium, and plant protein
- Weight gain, overweight or obesity
- Lower physical activity/fitness
- Sleep disturbances
- Psychosocial stressors
- Air pollution
2. **Recommendations for White-Coat Hypertension and Masked Hypertension**
- In adults with untreated office SBP ≥130 mm Hg or DBP ≥80 mm Hg, and without office SBP ≥160 mm Hg or DBP ≥100 mm Hg:
- Reasonable to exclude white-coat hypertension using out-of-office BP monitoring.
- In adults with white-coat hypertension or masked hypertension:
- Out-of-office BP monitoring is reasonable to exclude transition to a diagnosis of sustained hypertension.
- In adults with apparent treatment-resistant hypertension on office BP:
- Reasonable to exclude white-coat effect using out-of-office BP monitoring.
- In adults taking antihypertensive medication with elevated office BP:
- Reasonable to exclude white-coat effect using out-of-office BP monitoring.
- In adults with untreated office SBP &#x3C;130 mm Hg and DBP &#x3C;80 mm Hg:
- May be reasonable to exclude masked hypertension using out-of-office BP monitoring.
- In adults taking antihypertensive medication with office SBP &#x3C;130 mm Hg and DBP &#x3C;80 mm Hg:
- May be reasonable to exclude masked uncontrolled hypertension using out-of-office BP monitoring.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| BP Category                           | High BP in the Office Setting? | High BP Outside of the Office Setting? | Recommendation-Specific Supportive Text```markdown
|---------------------------------------|----------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Among individuals not taking antihypertensive medication | No                               | No                                      | 1. Systematic reviews and meta-analyses of observational studies have demonstrated that compared with sustained normotension, white-coat hypertension is associated with no risk to a moderately increased risk of CVD.1–5 The risk of CVD associated with white-coat hypertension may only be increased among older adults who have high baseline CVD risk.1,23 Nonetheless, the risk of CVD is higher in sustained hypertension than in white-coat hypertension among adults with high office BP.²⁴ Therefore, it is reasonable to exclude white-coat hypertension using out-of-office BP monitoring for adults with high office BP (ie, SBP/DBP ≥130/80 mm Hg). One caveat is that adults with office SBP/DBP ≥160/100 mm Hg should be promptly treated and antihypertensive medication dose titrated as necessary to control BP (Section 5.2, “Medical Management”). The prevalence of white-coat hypertension is low among those with office BP levels in this range.25,26 |
| Sustained normotension                | No                               | No                                      |                                                                                                  |
| Sustained hypertension                 | Yes                              | Yes                                     |                                                                                                  |
| Masked hypertension                   | No                               | Yes                                     |                                                                                                  |
| White-coat hypertension                | Yes                              | No                                      |                                                                                                  |
| Among individuals taking antihypertensive medication |                                  |                                         |                                                                                                  |
| Controlled hypertension                | No                               | No                                      | 2. Studies have demonstrated that compared with individuals with sustained normotension, a higher proportion of individuals with white-coat hypertension or masked hypertension have sustained hypertension during follow-up.6–8 An additional study²⁷ demonstrated that compared with individuals with sustained normotension, a higher proportion of individuals with white-coat hypertension had high out-of-office BP during follow-up. Therefore, it is reasonable to conduct out-of-office BP monitoring to exclude sustained hypertension among those initially identified with white-coat hypertension or masked hypertension. The frequency of follow-up monitoring is unclear as only a single visit was conducted at follow-up in these studies (ie, approximately 7 to 11 years after the baseline visit).6–8,27 |
| Uncontrolled hypertension              | Yes                              | Yes                                     |                                                                                                  |
| Masked uncontrolled hypertension       | No                               | Yes                                     |                                                                                                  |
| White-coat effect                     | Yes                              | No                                      | 3. Studies have consistently demonstrated that compared with controlled hypertension, white-coat effect is not associated with an increased risk of CVD events and mortality.9,11 Evidence also suggests that higher out-of-office BP is associated with an increased risk of CVD events, independent of office BP, among individuals with apparent resistant hypertension.10,12 Out-of-office BP monitoring is a central component of the initial work-up of apparent resistant hypertension (Section 5.6, “Resistant Hypertension and Renal Denervation”). Therefore, it is reasonable to exclude a white-coat effect using out-of-office BP monitoring for individuals with apparent resistant hypertension. |
```

2. Assess if the individual is taking antihypertensive medication

- If Uncontrolled Hypertension: High BP in both settings

- If White-coat Effect: High BP in office, no high BP out-of-office

- If Sustained Hypertension: High BP in both settings
- If Masked Hypertension: No high BP in office, high BP out-of-office
- If White-coat Hypertension: High BP in office, no high BP out-of-office

| CLINICAL STATEMENTS AND GUIDELINES | Prevalence | Indications for Additional Testing | Physical Examination Findings | Screening Tests | Confirmatory Tests |
|-------------------------------------|------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------------|------------------|--------------------|
| Common causes                       |            |                                   |                              |                  |                    |
| OSA5–7                              | 25%-50%    | Snoring, choking, gasping during sleep; daytime sleepiness; resistant hypertension | Obesity, large neck size (eg, >17 inches [men]; >16 inches [women], Mallampati class 3-4; loss of normal nocturnal BP fall | STOP-Bang Questionnaire¹⁵; Berlin Questionnaire¹⁶; overnight oximetry | Referral for polysomnography or home sleep apnea testing if no suspicion of nonrespiratory sleep disorders (eg, narcolepsy) |
| CKD17,18                            | 14%        | Diabetes, obstruction, hematuria; urinary frequency and nocturia; urinary incontinence, analgesic abuse; family history of polycystic kidney disease; elevated serum creatinine; abnormal urinalysis | Abdominal mass or large palpable kidneys (polycystic kidney disease); skin pallor | Electrolytes, including sodium, potassium, chloride, and bicarbonate, serum creatinine, urinalysis, urine microalbumin, serum cystatin C, renal ultrasound | Tests to evaluate cause of CKD |
| Primary aldosteronism1–3,9,19      | 5%-25%     | Resistant hypertension; hypertension with hypokalemia (spontaneous or diuretic induced); hypertension and muscle cramps or weakness; hypertension and incidentally discovered adrenal mass; hypertension and obstructive sleep apnea; hypertension and family history of early-onset hypertension or stroke | Arrhythmias (with hypokalemia); especially AF | Electrolytes, including sodium and potassium, plasma aldosterone/renin activity ratio (correction of hypokalemia and withdrawal of MRA for 4-6 wks) | Oral sodium loading test (with 24-h urine aldosterone) or IV saline infusion test with plasma aldosterone at 4 h of infusion or captopril suppression test (in patients not on ACEi or ARB treatment), adrenal CT scan, adrenal vein sampling |
| Drug or alcohol induced¹¹          | 2%-20%     | Sodium-containing antacids; antidepressants; nicotine (smoking); alcohol; NSAIDs; oral contraceptives; cyclosporine or tacrolimus; sympathomimetics (decongestants, anorectics); cocaine, amphetamines and other illicit drugs; neuropsychiatric agents; erythropoiesis-stimulating agents; cancer treatment (VEGF inhibitors, Bruton tyrosine kinase inhibitors and others), clonidine withdrawal; herbal agents (Ma Huang, ephedra) | Fine tremor, tachycardia, sweating (cocaine, ephedrine, MAO inhibitors); acute abdominal pain (cocaine) | Urinary drug screen (illicit drugs) | Response to withdrawal of suspected agent |
| Renovascular hypertension¹⁰        | 0.1%-5%    | Resistant hypertension; hypertension of abrupt onset or worsening or increasingly difficult to control; flash pulmonary edema (atherosclerotic); early-onset hypertension, especially in women (fibromuscular hyperplasia) | Abdominal systolic-diastolic bruit; bruits over other arteries (carotid, femoral) | Electrolytes, including sodium, potassium, chloride, and bicarbonate, renal duplex Doppler ultrasound; magnetic resonance arteriography; abdominal CT arteriography | Bilateral selective renal intra-arterial angiography |
| Uncommon causes                     |            |                                   |                              |                  |                    |
| Hypothyroidism²⁰                   | &#x3C;1%        | Dry skin; cold intolerance; constipation; hoarseness; weight gain | Delayed ankle reflex; periorbital edema; coarse skin; cold skin; slow movement; goiter | Thyroid-stimulating hormone; free thyroxine | None |
| Hyperthyroidism²⁰                  | &#x3C;1%        | Warm, moist skin; heat intolerance; nervousness; tremulousness; palpitations, insomnia; weight loss; diarrhea; proximal muscle weakness | Lid lag; fine tremor of the outstretched hands; warm, moist skin, goiter, thyroid nodule | Thyroid-stimulating hormone; free thyroxine | Radioactive iodine uptake and scan |
| Pheochromocytoma/paraganglioma21,22 | &#x3C;0.6%      | Resistant hypertension; paroxysmal hypertension or crisis superimposed on sustained hypertension; “spells,” BP lability, headache, sweating, palpitations, piloerection; positive family history of pheochromocytoma/paraganglioma; adrenal incidentaloma | Skin stigmata of neurofibromatosis (café-au-lait spots; neurofibromas); orthostatic hypotension | 24-h urinary fractionated metanephrines or plasma metanephrines under standard conditions (supine position with indwelling IV cannula) | CT or MRI scan of abdomen/pelvis, Ga-DOTATATE PET/CT scan |

```markdown
| Clinical Statements                     | Indications for Additional Testing                     | Physical Examination Findings                                                                 | Screening Tests                          | Confirmatory Tests                                         |
|-----------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|
| Aortic coarctation (undiagnosed or repaired)²³ | Young adult with hypertension (age &#x3C;30 y)            | BP higher in upper extremities than in lower extremities; absent femoral pulses; continuous murmur over patient’s back, chest, or abdominal bruit; left thoracotomy scar (postoperative) | Echocardiogram                           | Thoracic and abdominal CT angiogram or magnetic resonance arteriography |
| Cushing syndrome²⁴                     | Rapid weight gain, especially with central distribution; proximal muscle weakness; depression; hyperglycemia | Central obesity, “moon” face, dorsal and supraclavicular fat pads, wide (1 cm) violaceous striae, hirsutism | Overnight 1-mg dexamethasone suppression test | 24-h urinary free cortisol excretion (preferably multiple); midnight salivary cortisol |
| Primary hyperparathyroidism²⁰          | Hypercalcemia                                        | Usually none                                                                                   | Serum calcium                            | Serum parathyroid hormone                                  |
| Congenital adrenal hyperplasia²⁰       | Hypertension and hypokalemia; virilization (11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency [11-beta-OH]); incomplete masculinization in men and primary amenorrhea in women (17-alpha-hydroxylase deficiency [17-alpha-OH]) | Signs of virilization (11-beta-OH) or complete masculinization (17-alpha-OH)                  | Hypertension and hypokalemia with low or normal aldosterone and renin | 11-beta-OH: elevated DOC, 11-deoxycortisol, and androgens; 17-alpha-OH; decreased androgens and estrogen but elevated DOC and corticosterone |
| Mineralocorticoid excess syndromes other than primary aldosteronism²⁰ | Early-onset hypertension; resistant hypertension; hypokalemia or hyperkalemia | Arrhythmias (with hypokalemia)                                                                | Low aldosterone and renin                | Urinary cortisol metabolites; genetic testing             |
| Acromegaly²⁵                           | Acral features, enlarging shoe, glove, or hat size; headache, visual disturbances; diabetes | Acral features; large hands and feet; frontal bossing                                         | Serum growth hormone                      | Elevated age- and sex-matched IGF-1 level; MRI scan of the pituitary |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Patient presents with hypertension.
2. **Assess Age**
- If age &#x3C; 30 years, proceed to Aortic Coarctation evaluation.
- If age ≥ 30 years, proceed to next step.
3. **Evaluate Symptoms**
- Check for symptoms of Cushing syndrome (rapid weight gain, central obesity, etc.).
- If present, conduct screening for Cushing syndrome.
- If absent, proceed to next step.
4. **Check for Hypercalcemia**
- If hypercalcemia is present, evaluate for Primary Hyperparathyroidism.
- If absent, proceed to next step.
5. **Assess for Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia**
- Check for hypertension and hypokalemia.
- If present, conduct screening for Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia.
- If absent, proceed to next step.
6. **Evaluate for Mineralocorticoid Excess Syndromes**
- Check for early-onset hypertension and hypokalemia.
- If present, conduct screening for Mineralocorticoid Excess Syndromes.
- If absent, proceed to next step.
7. **Assess for Acromegaly**
- Check for acral features and other symptoms.
- If present, conduct screening for Acromegaly.
- If absent, consider other causes of hypertension.
8. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

| COR | LOE  | Recommendations                                                                                                           |
|-----|------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | C-LD | In adults with an indication for screening for primary aldosteronism, use of plasma aldosterone, renin activity, and the plasma aldosterone to renin activity ratio is recommended for initial screening to assess if there is biochemical evidence of primary aldosteronism. |
| 1   | C-EO | In adults with hypertension and a positive screening test for primary aldosteronism or continued suspicion for primary aldosteronism based on suppressed plasma renin or disproportionate target organ damage, referral to a hypertension specialist or endocrinologist is recommended for further evaluation and treatment. |
| 1   | C-EO | In adults with an indication for screening for primary aldosteronism, it is recommended to continue most antihypertensive medications (other than mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists [MRAs]) prior to initial screening to minimize barriers to or delays in screening. |

### Sơ đồ/Lưu đồ

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Does the patient have any of the following conditions associated with secondary HTN?**
- Drug-resistant/induced HTN
- Unprovoked or excessive hypokalemia
- Abrupt onset of HTN
- Onset of HTN at &#x3C;30 y
- Exacerbation of previously controlled HTN
- Disproportionate TOD for degree of HTN
- Accelerated/malignant HTN
- Onset of diastolic HTN in older adults (age ≥65 y)
- Concomitant adrenal nodule
- History of early-onset stroke
- Family history of primary aldosteronism
- **YES**: Screen for primary aldosteronism and other secondary forms of HTN
- **NO**: Screening not indicated

2. **Positive screening test?**
- **YES**: Refer to clinician with specific secondary HTN expertise
- **NO**: Enhance medication therapy
[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
| Agent                                                   | Possible Management Strategy                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
|---------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Nonprescription drugs/substance**                     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Alcohol                                                 | Options include abstinence or limit alcohol to ≤1 drink daily for women and ≤2 drinks daily for men.                                                                                                                         |
| Caffeine²⁸                                            | Limit caffeine intake to &#x3C;300 mg/d. Avoid more than 1 cup daily in patients with severe uncontrolled hypertension.                                                                                                           |
| Decongestants (eg, phenylephrine, pseudoephedrine)     | Use for shortest duration possible and avoid in severe or uncontrolled hypertension. Consider alternative therapies (eg, nasal saline, intranasal corticosteroids, antihistamines) as appropriate.                               |
| Herbal supplements (eg, Ma Huang, ephedra, St. John’s wort [with MAO inhibitors, yohimbine]) | Avoid use.                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Black licorice²⁹                                       | Avoid use.                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| NSAIDs; acetaminophen                                   | Avoid systemic NSAIDs when possible. Limit acetaminophen to less than 4 g/d. Consider alternative analgesics (eg, topical NSAIDs), depending on indication and risk.                                                            |
| Recreational drugs (eg, “bath salts” [MDPV], cocaine, methamphetamine, etc) | Discontinue or avoid use.                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| **Prescription drugs**                                  |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Sudden withdrawal of central-acting sympatholytic drugs such as clonidine and tizanidine | Recommend avoiding oral clonidine for treatment of hypertension whenever possible and tapering upon discontinuation; use cyclobenzaprine or other muscle relaxants instead of tizanidine.                                      |
| Amphetamines† (eg, amphetamine, methylphenidate, dexmethylphenidate, dexamfetamine, lisdexamfetamine, dextroamphetamine) | Discontinue or decrease dose. Consider behavioral therapies or nonstimulants (such as guanfacine) for ADHD.                                                                                                                  |
| Antidepressants† (eg, MAOIs, SNRIs, TCAs)              | Consider alternative agents (eg, SSRIs) depending on indication. Avoid tyramine-containing foods with MAOIs.                                                                                                                 |
| Atypical antipsychotics† (eg, risperidone, olanzapine) | Discontinue or limit use when possible. Consider behavior therapy where appropriate.                                                                                                                                         |
|                                                         | Recommend lifestyle modification (Section 5.1 “Lifestyle and Psychosocial Approaches”). Consider alternative agents associated with lower risk of weight gain, diabetes, and dyslipidemia.                                   |
| Immunosuppressants† (eg, cyclosporine)                 | Consider converting to tacrolimus, which may be associated with fewer effects on BP.                                                                                                                                       |
| Oral contraceptives†                                   | Use low-dose (eg, 20-30 mcg ethinyl estradiol) agents or a progestin-only form of contraception, or consider alternative forms of birth control where appropriate (eg, barrier, abstinence, nonhormonal IUD). Avoid use in women with uncontrolled hypertension. |
| Systemic corticosteroids† (eg, dexamethasone, fludrocortisone, methylprednisolone, prednisone, prednisolone) | Avoid or limit use when possible. Consider alternative modes of administration (eg, inhaled, topical) when feasible.                                                                                                         |
| Angiogenesis inhibitor† (eg, bevacizumab) and tyrosine kinase inhibitors (eg, sunitinib, sorafenib) | Avoid or limit use when possible.                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Androgen deprivation therapy† such as CYP 17 inhibitors (eg, abiraterone, orteronel) or androgen receptor antagonist (eg, enzalutamide) | Avoid or limit use when possible. Consider alternative chemotherapy.                                                                                                                                                          |
```

[START_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
| **Clinical Statements and Guidelines**                                                                                          |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| For sodium and volume status, is relatively autonomous at a young age may have primary aldosteronism due to glucocorticoid-remediable aldosteronism (familial hyperaldosteronism type-1) and therefore also warrant screening. However, the rate of screening for primary aldosteronism in guideline-recommended individuals is &#x3C;2% in the United States. |
| Growing evidence supports that primary aldosteronism occurs across the full breadth of hypertension severity, with higher prevalence of primary aldosteronism as the severity of hypertension increases. The prevalence of primary aldosteronism is approximately 5% to 10% among individuals with stage 1 hypertension and 11% to 22% among individuals with stage 2 hypertension, which varies depending on the modality of testing and testing thresholds used to diagnose primary aldosteronism. The prevalence of primary aldosteronism may be similar among individuals with stage 2 hypertension and those with resistant hypertension. Studies from Australia and Japan suggest that broader screening for primary aldosteronism in individuals with hypertension is cost-effective. Nonetheless, the potential burden of additional testing outside of patients with resistant hypertension to confirm the diagnosis of primary aldosteronism and to determine optimal therapy may be taken into consideration prior to screening. |
| Patients with primary aldosteronism are at greater risk for target organ damage than those with primary hypertension due to the toxic tissue effects of aldosterone even when adjusted for degree of hypertension. Meta-analyses of studies that matched patients with primary aldosteronism to those with primary hypertension showed that primary aldosteronism carries a 2.0-fold increased risk of HF, 2.8-fold increased risk of stroke, 1.7-fold increased risk of coronary artery disease, 4.0-fold increased risk of AF, and increased kidney damage compared with primary hypertension. Because the deleterious effects of aldosterone overproduction may be blocked with unilateral laparoscopic adrenalectomy or treatment with MRA (eg, spironolactone or eplerenone), patients with hypertension at increased risk of primary aldosteronism are very likely to benefit from screening. These include patients with hypertension and adrenal “incidentaloma,” an incidentally discovered adrenal lesion on computed tomography or magnetic resonance imaging performed for other purposes. Additionally, patients with resistant hypertension (regardless of whether hypokalemia is present), hypertension with hypokalemia (either spontaneous or diuretic-induced), and hypertension with OSA have a relatively high prevalence of primary aldosteronism (∼20%-35%). Patients with hypertension and a history of early-onset hypertension and/or cerebrovascular accident should also be considered for screening. |
| The combined interpretation of the plasma aldosterone concentration, renin activity, and aldosterone to renin activity ratio is currently the most accurate and reliable means of screening for primary aldosteronism. Patients with primary aldosteronism typically have suppressed renin activity (&#x3C;1 ng/mL/h). Most data support that the plasma aldosterone concentration should be at least 10 ng/dL to interpret the test as positive, but additional evaluation may be indicated if the renin activity is suppressed. The most commonly used cutoff value for the aldosterone to renin activity ratio is 30 when plasma aldosterone concentration is reported in nanograms per deciliter (ng/dL) and plasma renin activity in nanograms per milliliter per hour (ng/mL/h), although some data support alternative thresholds (20 or 40). Patients should have unrestricted salt intake, serum potassium in the normal range (to avoid false-negative testing), and ideally, MRA (eg, spironolactone or eplerenone) withdrawn for at least 4 weeks before testing. |
| Low screening rates may be due to several barriers to screening, such as older guidance to withdraw antihypertensive medications that may affect renin and aldosterone levels, which has since been disputed by growing evidence in support of screening despite treatment with these medications. Specifically, beta blockers (BBs) and central-alpha agonists can suppress both renin and aldosterone. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Screening for Primary Aldosteronism**
- **Step 1:** Identify patients with hypertension.
- **Branch A:** Patients with adrenal incidentaloma.
- **Branch B:** Patients with resistant hypertension.
- **Branch C:** Patients with hypertension and hypokalemia.
- **Branch D:** Patients with hypertension and OSA.
- **Branch E:** Patients with early-onset hypertension or cerebrovascular accident.

- **Step 2:** Assess risk factors for primary aldosteronism.
- **Branch A:** Check for family history of hyperaldosteronism.
- **Branch B:** Evaluate severity of hypertension.

- **Step 3:** Conduct laboratory tests.
- **Test A:** Measure plasma aldosterone concentration.
- **Test B:** Measure plasma renin activity.
- **Test C:** Calculate aldosterone to renin activity ratio.

- **Step 4:** Interpret results.
- **If aldosterone concentration ≥ 10 ng/dL and renin activity suppressed:**
- **Positive for primary aldosteronism.**
- **If aldosterone to renin ratio ≥ 30:**
- **Confirm diagnosis.**
- **If results are inconclusive:**
- **Consider further evaluation.**

- **Step 5:** Determine treatment options.
- **Option A:** Unilateral laparoscopic adrenalectomy.
- **Option B:** Medical therapy with MRA (spironolactone or eplerenone).
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

```markdown
| **Clinical Statements and Guidelines**                                                                                     | | |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---|---|
| Angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors (ACEis) and angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs) may stimulate renin and suppress aldosterone; thiazide-type, loop diuretics, MRA, and potassium-sparing diuretics can stimulate both renin and aldosterone. Throughout this guideline, we use the term thiazide- to collectively refer to hydrochlorothiazide (HCTZ), chlorthalidone, and indapamide. Although the literature traditionally differentiates these medications based on their chemical structures, categorizing HCTZ as a thiazide-type diuretic because it possesses a benzothiadiazine ring and categorizing chlorthalidone and indapamide as thiazide-like diuretics because they lack this ring (yet remain closely related sulfonamide derivatives), the interchangeability of thiazide-type and -like agents remains a debated topic. For simplicity and to minimize confusion, in this guideline we have chosen to group them under a single term. In most settings, it is acceptable for clinicians to choose among these agents for treatment. We recognize that there are differences in potency and half-life between these agents that may be relevant in some situations, particularly in the management of resistant hypertension. Initial screening for primary aldosteronism can typically be interpreted in the context of most medications that affect renin and aldosterone. If screening results are negative or borderline in patients in whom there is a high level of suspicion for primary aldosteronism and confirmation of the diagnosis will change management, potentially interfering medications may be temporarily substituted with noninterfering medications for at least 2 to 4 weeks prior to repeat testing. The diagnosis of primary aldosteronism may require an aldosterone suppression test such as an intravenous (IV) saline suppression test or oral salt-loading test. If the diagnosis of primary aldosteronism is confirmed and the patient agrees that surgery would be desirable, the patient is referred for an adrenal venous sampling procedure to determine whether the increased aldosterone production is unilateral or bilateral in origin. If unilateral excess aldosterone production is documented on adrenal venous sampling, the patient is referred for unilateral laparoscopic adrenalectomy. If the patient has bilaterally increased aldosterone secretion on adrenal venous sampling, is not a surgical candidate, or is not interested in pursuing surgery, the patient is treated with an MRA (e.g., spironolactone or eplerenone). If primary aldosteronism is confirmed, imaging of the adrenal glands should be considered, even if treatment will be with medication, to exclude a large adrenal mass that may require surgical intervention. | | |
| **Recommendations for Renal Artery Stenosis**                                                                             | | |
| **COR** | **LOE** | **Recommendations**                                                                                                   |
|---------|---------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1       | A       | In adults with hypertension and atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis, medical therapy is recommended to reduce kidney and CVD morbidity and mortality. |
| 2a      | C-EO    | In adults with hypertension and atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis for whom medical management has failed (e.g., resistant hypertension, worsening kidney function, and/or acute HF), it is reasonable to refer patients for revascularization by percutaneous renal artery angioplasty and/or stent placement. |
| 2b      | C-LD    | In adults with hypertension and nonatherosclerotic renal artery stenosis, including fibromuscular dysplasia, it may be reasonable to refer patients for revascularization by percutaneous renal artery angioplasty. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Initial Screening for Primary Aldosteronism**
- If positive or high suspicion: Confirm diagnosis
- If negative or borderline: Consider temporary substitution of medications

2. **Diagnosis Confirmation**
- Aldosterone suppression test (IV saline or oral salt-loading)
- If confirmed:
- **Adrenal Venous Sampling**
- If unilateral excess aldosterone: Refer for unilateral laparoscopic adrenalectomy
- If bilateral excess aldosterone:
- If surgical candidate: Discuss surgery
- If not surgical candidate or not interested: Treat with MRA (e.g., spironolactone or eplerenone)

3. **Imaging of Adrenal Glands**
- Consider imaging to exclude large adrenal mass if primary aldosteronism is confirmed

4. **Renal Artery Stenosis Management**
- If atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis:
- Medical therapy recommended
- If medical management fails: Refer for revascularization
- If nonatherosclerotic renal artery stenosis: Consider referral for revascularization
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Markdown Table

| Clinical Statements                                                                 | Recommendation-Specific Supportive Text                                                                 |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Atherosclerotic disease in the renal arteries represents systemic disease and indicates higher risk for both kidney failure and cardiovascular morbidity and mortality. No RCT to date has demonstrated a clear clinical advantage of renal artery revascularization (with either angioplasty or stenting) over medical therapy among individuals with hypertension. | Lifestyle interventions are recommended for all individuals with high BP (Section 5.1, “Lifestyle and Psychosocial Approaches”), including adults with OSA and hypertension. Obesity is a risk factor for OSA. A meta-analysis examining the impact of weight loss interventions on BP in adults with OSA demonstrated small effects on SBP (−1.86 mm Hg; 95% CI: −3.57 to −0.15 mm Hg) and DBP (−2.07 mm Hg; 95% CI: −3.79 to −0.35 mm Hg). However, weight loss interventions when combined with CPAP therapy have been shown to lower SBP by 8 mm Hg in adults with moderate-to-severe OSA. Recent data from SURMOUNT OSA (Tirzepatide for the Treatment of OSA) demonstrated that tirzepatide versus placebo was associated with a reduction in BP at 48 weeks, a prespecified key secondary endpoint, among adults with moderate-to-severe OSA and obesity. For adults not on CPAP therapy, the estimated treatment difference for SBP was −7.6 mm Hg (95% CI: −10.5 to −4.8 mm Hg) and for DBP was −2.8 mm Hg (95% CI: −5.0 to −0.6 mm Hg); whereas for adults on CPAP therapy the estimated treatment difference for SBP was −3.7 mm Hg (95% CI: −6.8 to −0.7 mm Hg) and for DBP was −1.1 mm Hg (95% CI: −3.2 to 1.0 mm Hg). |
| Revascularization can be favorable for select individuals with uncontrolled hypertension accompanied by progressively worsening kidney function and/or acute HF who were not represented in RCTs. |                                                                                                          |
| Revascularization may be considered for those with nonatherosclerotic renal artery disease (eg, fibromuscular dysplasia, Takayasu arteritis in select cases) and hypertension and may be curative. Fibromuscular dysplasia is most common in women (90%) and may present at a younger age (mean age 53 years) with almost equal frequency in the renal and carotid circulations. Percutaneous transluminal angioplasty alone without stenting can improve BP control and even normalize BP, especially in patients with recent onset of hypertension or resistant hypertension. |                                                                                                          |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Assess for hypertension and OSA.
- Determine if the patient is overweight or obese.
- **Yes**: Proceed to weight loss interventions combined with CPAP treatment.
- **No**: Continue with standard hypertension management.
2. **Weight Loss Interventions**
- Monitor BP response.
- Evaluate effectiveness of CPAP therapy.
- **Effective**: Continue CPAP and monitor BP.
- **Not Effective**: Consider alternative treatments.
3. **Resistant Hypertension**
- Assess if the patient has moderate-to-severe OSA.
- **Yes**: Initiate CPAP treatment.
- **No**: Explore other antihypertensive options.
4. **Monitor BP**
- Evaluate BP outcomes based on adherence to CPAP therapy and OSA severity.
5. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Markdown Table

| COR | LOE | Recommendations                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
|-----|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 4   | 2a  | In adults with or without hypertension, potassium-based salt substitutes can be useful to prevent or treat elevated BP and hypertension, particularly for patients in whom salt intake is related mostly to food preparation or flavoring at home, except in the presence of CKD or use of drugs that reduce potassium excretion where monitoring of serum potassium levels is indicated. |
| 5   | 1   | In adults with elevated BP or hypertension, moderate potassium supplementation, ideally from dietary sources, is recommended to prevent or treat elevated BP and hypertension, except in the presence of CKD or use of drugs that reduce potassium excretion where monitoring of serum potassium levels is indicated. |
| 6   | 1   | Adults with or without hypertension who currently consume alcohol should be advised to pursue a recommended goal of abstinence, or at least to reduce alcohol intake to ≤1 drink/d for women and ≤2 drinks/d for men to prevent or treat elevated BP and hypertension. |
| 7   | 1   | In adults with or without hypertension, increasing physical activity, through a structured exercise program that includes aerobic exercise and/or resistance training, is recommended to prevent or treat elevated BP and hypertension. |
| 8   | 2b  | In adults with or without hypertension, stress reduction through transcendental meditation may be reasonable to prevent or treat elevated BP and hypertension, as an adjunct to lifestyle or medication interventions. |
| 9   | 2b  | In adults with or without hypertension, other forms of stress management, such as breathing control techniques or yoga, may be reasonable to prevent or treat elevated BP and hypertension, as an adjunct to lifestyle or medication interventions. |
| 1   | A   | In adults who have overweight or obesity, weight loss is recommended with a goal of at least 5% of body weight reduction to prevent or treat elevated BP and hypertension. |
| 1   | A   | In adults with or without hypertension, a heart-healthy eating pattern, such as the DASH eating plan, is recommended to prevent or treat elevated BP and hypertension. |
| 1   | A   | In adults with or without hypertension, reduction of dietary sodium intake is recommended to &#x3C;2300 mg/d, moving toward an ideal limit of &#x3C;1500 mg/d to prevent or treat elevated BP and hypertension. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Assess patient for hypertension.
- Determine if the patient has overweight or obesity.
- Evaluate lifestyle factors (diet, alcohol consumption, physical activity, stress management).

2. **Weight Management**
- If overweight/obese: Recommend weight loss (≥5% body weight).
- If not overweight/obese: Proceed to dietary recommendations.

3. **Dietary Recommendations**
- Recommend heart-healthy eating pattern (e.g., DASH).
- Advise reduction of dietary sodium intake (&#x3C;2300 mg/d, ideally &#x3C;1500 mg/d).

4. **Potassium Management**
- Assess potassium intake and supplementation.
- If elevated BP or hypertension: Recommend moderate potassium supplementation from dietary sources.

5. **Alcohol Consumption**
- If consuming alcohol: Advise reduction to ≤1 drink/d for women and ≤2 drinks/d for men.
- If abstinent: Proceed to physical activity recommendations.

6. **Physical Activity**
- Recommend structured exercise program (aerobic and/or resistance training).

7. **Stress Management**
- Recommend stress reduction techniques (e.g., transcendental meditation, yoga).

8. **Follow-Up**
- Monitor BP and adjust recommendations as necessary.
[END_FLOWCHART]

[START_FLOWCHART]

2. **Assess Patient's Blood Pressure**

### Lưu đồ

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**
- Đánh giá tình trạng huyết áp của bệnh nhân.
2. **Có huyết áp cao?**
- Có: Tiến hành các bước tiếp theo.
- Không: Khuyến nghị lối sống lành mạnh.
3. **Đánh giá chỉ số BMI**
- BMI ≥ 25: Khuyến nghị giảm cân.
- BMI &#x3C; 25: Tiến hành các can thiệp khác.
4. **Giảm cân?**
- Có: Theo dõi huyết áp.
- Không: Cân nhắc điều trị bằng thuốc.
5. **Giảm natri?**
- Có: Theo dõi huyết áp.
- Không: Khuyến nghị thay thế muối natri bằng muối giàu kali.
6. **Theo dõi huyết áp**
- Đánh giá hiệu quả của các can thiệp.
7. **Kết thúc**
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá tình trạng huyết áp của bệnh nhân.
- Nếu huyết áp không kiểm soát:
- Tiến hành các biện pháp can thiệp.
- **Giảm tiêu thụ muối**: Sử dụng thay thế muối (75% natri clorua/25% kali clorua).
- Kết quả: Giảm SBP 3.3 mm Hg.
- **Giảm tiêu thụ rượu**:
- Nếu uống ≥4 ly/ngày: Giảm ít nhất 50% lượng rượu.
- Kết quả: Giảm huyết áp 5.5/4.0 mm Hg.
- **Tăng cường hoạt động thể chất**:
- Khuyến khích tập thể dục nhịp điệu (jogging, aerobic).
- Kết quả: Giảm huyết áp 4 đến 7/3 đến 4 mm Hg.
- Tập thể dục kháng lực.
- Kết quả: Giảm huyết áp 3/2 mm Hg.
- Tập thể dục tĩnh.
- Kết quả: Giảm huyết áp khoảng 8/4 mm Hg.
- Nếu huyết áp vẫn không kiểm soát:
- Xem xét bổ sung kali.
- Kết quả: Giảm huyết áp 6/4 mm Hg.
2. **Kết thúc**: Đánh giá lại huyết áp sau can thiệp.
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Markdown Table

| COR | LOE | Recommendations                                                                                                           |
|-----|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | A   | In all adults with hypertension, initiation of medications to lower BP is recommended when average SBP is ≥140 mm Hg to reduce the risk of cardiovascular events and total mortality.1–6 |
| 2   | A   | In all adults with hypertension, initiation of medications to lower BP is recommended when average DBP is ≥90 mm Hg to reduce the risk of cardiovascular events and total mortality.1–6 |
| 3   | A   | In adults with hypertension and clinical CVD, initiation of medications to lower BP is recommended when average SBP is ≥130 mm Hg to reduce the risk of cardiovascular events and total mortality.5–8 |
| 4   | C-LD| In adults with hypertension and clinical CVD, initiation of medications to lower BP is recommended when average DBP is ≥80 mm Hg to reduce the risk of cardiovascular events and total mortality.5–8 |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Initiation of Pharmacologic BP Treatment**
- Context: Overall CVD Risk
- Evidence from meta-analyses and observational studies shows benefits of antihypertensive therapy.
- Prioritize those at highest cardiovascular risk using absolute risk estimation.
- Consideration of public health burden at stage 1 hypertension.
- BP Lowering Treatment Trialists’ Collaboration findings.
- Risk-based strategy for therapy is more effective than BP-alone based strategy.

2. **Stress-Reduction Strategies**
- Various strategies assessed for BP lowering.
- Moderate- to high-level evidence for transcendental meditation.
- Mean reductions of approximately 5/2 mm Hg in SBP/DBP.
- Meditation less effective than lifestyle interventions (DASH, structured exercise, low-sodium/higher-potassium intake).
- Heterogeneous study designs and small trial sizes noted.

3. **Other Interventions**
- Breathing control interventions lower SBP/DBP by approximately 5/3 mm Hg.
- Low- to moderate-grade evidence for yoga lowering BP.
- Quantitative CVD risk estimation improves antihypertensive therapy initiation benefits.

4. **Medical Management**
- Thiazide-type diuretics include HCTZ, chlorthalidone, and indapamide.
- Grouping for simplicity despite chemical structure differences.
- Treatment recommendations for resistant hypertension favor thiazide-like diuretics.

5. **BP Treatment Threshold**
- Recommendations for BP treatment thresholds based on CVD risk estimation.
- Initiation of medications recommended based on average SBP and DBP thresholds.
[END_FLOWCHART]
