```markdown
| COR | LOE  | Recommendations                                                                                                           |
|-----|------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | A    | In adults with hypertension without clinical CVD but with diabetes or CKD or at increased short-term CVD risk (ie, estimated 10-year CVD risk ≥7.5% based on PREVENT*), initiation of medications to lower BP is recommended when average SBP is ≥130 mm Hg to reduce the risk of CVD events and total mortality. |
| 1   | C-LD | In adults with hypertension without clinical CVD but with diabetes or CKD or at increased 10-year CVD risk (ie, ≥7.5% based on PREVENT*), initiation of medications to lower BP is recommended when average DBP is ≥80 mm Hg to reduce the risk of CVD events and total mortality. |
| 1   | B-R  | In adults with hypertension without clinical CVD and with estimated 10-year CVD risk &#x3C;7.5% based on PREVENT,* initiation of medications to lower BP is recommended if average SBP remains ≥130 mm Hg after a 3- to 6-month trial of lifestyle intervention to prevent target organ damage and mitigate further rise in BP. |
| 1   | B-R  | In adults with hypertension without clinical CVD and with estimated 10-year CVD risk &#x3C;7.5% based on PREVENT,* initiation of medications to lower BP is recommended if average DBP ≥80 mm Hg after a 3- to 6-month trial of lifestyle intervention to prevent target organ damage and mitigate further rise in BP. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**: Assess adult with hypertension.
- Is there clinical CVD?
- Yes: Proceed to step 2.
- No: Proceed to step 3.

2. **Clinical CVD Present**:
- Is average SBP ≥130 mm Hg?
- Yes: Initiate antihypertensive therapy.
- No: Monitor and reassess.

3. **No Clinical CVD**:
- Is there diabetes, CKD, or estimated 10-year CVD risk ≥7.5%?
- Yes: Proceed to step 4.
- No: Proceed to step 5.

4. **Increased Risk**:
- Is average SBP ≥130 mm Hg?
- Yes: Initiate antihypertensive therapy.
- No: Monitor lifestyle changes for 3-6 months.

5. **Low Risk**:
- Is average SBP ≥130 mm Hg after lifestyle intervention?
- Yes: Initiate antihypertensive therapy.
- No: Continue monitoring.

**End**: Treatment decision made based on risk assessment and BP levels.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**: Assess patient's average BP.
- Is average BP ≥140/90 mm Hg?
- **YES**: Initiate antihypertensive medications to lower BP and reduce CVD risk for primary or secondary prevention of CVD.
- **NO**: Proceed to next question.

2. **Risk-Based Thresholds**: Does the patient have existing clinical CVD (CHD, stroke, HF)?
- **YES**: Initiate antihypertensive medications to lower BP and reduce CVD risk if average SBP ≥130 mm Hg or DBP ≥80 mm Hg for secondary prevention of CVD.
- **NO**: Proceed to next question.

3. Does the patient have diabetes or CKD, or is the patient at increased short-term risk of CVD (10-year PREVENT-CVD risk ≥7.5%)?
- **YES**: Initiate antihypertensive medications to lower BP and reduce CVD risk if average SBP ≥130 mm Hg or DBP ≥80 mm Hg for primary prevention of CVD.
- **NO**: Proceed to next step.

4. Initiate antihypertensive medications to lower BP if average SBP ≥130 mm Hg or DBP ≥80 mm Hg after 3-6 months of lifestyle intervention.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

### Bảng biểu

| STT | Nội dung                                                                                                           |
|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | Jones et al 2025 High Blood Pressure Guideline                                                                    |
| 2   | Clinical Statements and Guidelines                                                                                 |
| 3   | Prerandomization average DBP of 84 mm Hg from 48 RCTs among 157,728 patients with previous CVD who demonstrated benefit with initiation of antihypertensive therapy for BP lowering. |
| 4   | In adults without clinical CVD who are at lower 10-year predicted CVD risk based on PREVENT (&#x3C;7.5%), there are limited data about the net benefit of initiation of antihypertensive therapy at a lower threshold with an average SBP ≥130 mm Hg. |
| 5   | Lifestyle interventions should be encouraged first to lower BP (Section 5.1, “Lifestyle and Psychosocial Approaches”). |
| 6   | If average SBP is ≥130 mm Hg after a 3- to 6-month trial, initiation of antihypertensive therapy is advised as an adjunct to lifestyle interventions. |
| 7   | In the SPRINT trial, increased predicted risk of CVD of ≥15% based on the Framingham risk score was an inclusion criterion. |
| 8   | 76% of enrolled participants had a Framingham 10-year estimated CVD risk ≥15%.                                   |
| 9   | In the STEP trial, 65% of participants had a Framingham 10-year estimated CVD risk ≥15%.                         |
| 10  | Initiation of antihypertensive treatment for adults aged ≥80 years is recommended at ≥130/80 mm Hg when clinical judgment suggests benefits will outweigh harms. |
| 11  | Data from observational cohorts demonstrate lower risk of subclinical CVD among those with BP &#x3C;130/80 mm Hg.      |
| 12  | Having a history of hypertensive disorders of pregnancy may identify individuals who have higher long-term predicted risk. |

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá tình trạng bệnh nhân.
2. **Có CVD không?**
- Có: **Khuyến nghị điều trị antihypertensive.**
- Không: **Tiếp tục đánh giá nguy cơ CVD.**
3. **Nguy cơ CVD 10 năm dự đoán?**
- &#x3C;7.5%: **Khuyến nghị can thiệp lối sống.**
- ≥7.5%: **Khởi đầu điều trị antihypertensive nếu SBP ≥130 mm Hg.**
4. **SBP sau 3-6 tháng can thiệp lối sống?**
- &#x3C;130 mm Hg: **Tiếp tục theo dõi.**
- ≥130 mm Hg: **Khởi đầu điều trị antihypertensive.**
5. **Tuổi ≥80?**
- Có: **Khởi đầu điều trị nếu lợi ích vượt trội hơn rủi ro.**
- Không: **Tiếp tục theo dõi và đánh giá.**
6. **Kết thúc**: Đánh giá lại và điều chỉnh điều trị theo nhu cầu.
[END_FLOWCHART]

---

### Bảng biểu

| COR | LOE | Recommendation                                                                                                           |
|-----|-----|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | A   | For adults initiating antihypertensive drug therapy, thiazide-type diuretics, long-acting dihydropyridine CCB, and ACEi or ARB are recommended as first-line therapy to prevent CVD.1,2 |
| 1   | B-R | In adults with stage 2 hypertension (SBP ≥ 140 mm Hg and DBP ≥ 90 mm Hg), initiation of antihypertensive drug therapy with 2 first-line agents of different classes, ideally in a single-pill combination (SPC), is recommended to improve BP control and adherence.1–6 |
| 2a  | C-EO| In adults with stage 1 hypertension (SBP 130-139 mm Hg and DBP 80-89 mm Hg), initiation of antihypertensive drug therapy with a single first-line antihypertensive drug is reasonable, with dosage titration and sequential addition of other agents as needed to achieve BP control. |
| 3: Harm | A | Simultaneous use of an ACEi, ARB, and/or renin inhibitor in combination is not recommended due to the potential for harm.7–9 |

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu điều trị tăng huyết áp**
- Xem xét các yếu tố đồng mắc (ví dụ: bệnh mạch vành, suy tim, đột quỵ, tiểu đường, CKD).
- Chọn thuốc hạ huyết áp dựa trên các yếu tố đồng mắc.

2. **Chọn thuốc điều trị ban đầu**
- Nếu bệnh nhân ≥30 tuổi và DBP ≥80 mm Hg sau 3-6 tháng thử nghiệm lối sống:
- Bắt đầu điều trị bằng thuốc hạ huyết áp.
- Lựa chọn thuốc: thiazide-type diuretics, long-acting dihydropyridine CCB, ACEi hoặc ARB.

3. **Đánh giá mức độ huyết áp**
- Nếu huyết áp vẫn cao (SBP ≥ 140 mm Hg và DBP ≥ 90 mm Hg):
- Khuyến nghị sử dụng 2 loại thuốc hạ huyết áp khác nhau, tốt nhất là trong một viên kết hợp (SPC).

4. **Nếu huyết áp ở mức độ 1 (SBP 130-139 mm Hg và DBP 80-89 mm Hg)**
- Khuyến nghị bắt đầu điều trị với một loại thuốc hạ huyết áp đơn lẻ.
- Tăng liều hoặc thêm thuốc khác nếu cần thiết để đạt được kiểm soát huyết áp.

5. **Kiểm tra tương tác thuốc**
- Không khuyến nghị sử dụng đồng thời ACEi, ARB và/hoặc renin inhibitor do nguy cơ gây hại.
[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Assess patient for hypertension.
- Determine if the patient has any contraindications or comorbidities.

2. **Initial Treatment Decision**
- Is the patient at high risk for cardiovascular events?
- Yes: Consider starting with a combination therapy.
- No: Proceed to monotherapy options.

3. **Monotherapy Options**
- Choose from the following classes:
- Diuretics
- Beta blockers
- ACE inhibitors
- ARBs
- Calcium channel blockers
- Assess patient's response to the chosen medication.

4. **Combination Therapy Consideration**
- If monotherapy is ineffective, consider adding a second agent.
- Possible combinations:
- Diuretic + ACE inhibitor
- Diuretic + ARB
- Beta blocker + Diuretic
- Calcium channel blocker + ACE inhibitor

5. **Monitor and Adjust Treatment**
- Regularly monitor blood pressure and adjust medications as necessary.
- Consider referral to a specialist if hypertension remains uncontrolled.

6. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Start with patient assessment for hypertension.
- If stage 1 hypertension:
- Consider single agent therapy.
- If stage 2 hypertension or high-risk patients:
- Initiate combination therapy with 2 agents from different classes.
- Preferably use a single pill combination (SPC) to improve adherence.
- Monitor for hypotension or orthostatic hypotension in older patients.
- If BP is not adequately controlled, consider adding another agent.
2. If using stepped-care approach:
- Start with a single agent.
- Titrate dose and add other agents as needed.
- Ensure regular follow-up to assess BP control and medication efficacy.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

### Markdown Table

| COR | LOE | Recommendations                                                                                                           |
|-----|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | B-R | 1. In adults with hypertension, antihypertensive medication dosing once daily rather than multiple times daily is beneficial to improve medication adherence.1–3 |
|     |     | 2. In adults with hypertension, the use of a SPC to reduce pill burden rather than taking separate pills is effective to improve medication adherence.4–9 |
|     |     | 3. In adults with hypertension, use of medication reminder aids and educational or self-management interventions can be useful to improve medication adherence.10–16 |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Identify Nonadherence**
- Assess for barriers to adherence in a nonjudgmental manner.
- Create a plan that includes patient preferences and shared decision-making.

2. **Screen for Mental Health Issues**
- Screen for stress, anxiety, and depression using valid scales.
- Refer for appropriate interventions if issues are identified.

3. **Screen for Health Literacy**
- Conduct screening for low health literacy.
- Provide additional education and resources if low health literacy is identified.

4. **Medication Dosing Strategy**
- Recommend once-daily dosing to improve adherence.
- Evaluate the impact of dosing frequency on adherence.

5. **Simplification of Regimens**
- Use single pill combinations (SPC) to reduce pill burden.
- Consider medication synchronization to improve adherence.

6. **Utilize Reminder Aids**
- Implement medication reminder aids (e.g., apps, texts).
- Educate patients on self-management strategies.

7. **Monitor Adherence**
- Use various methods to assess adherence (self-report, pill counts, etc.).
- Provide feedback to clinicians about adherence levels.

8. **Adjust Therapy as Needed**
- Modify drug therapy regimens based on adherence assessments.
- Simplify regimens to enhance adherence.
[END_FLOWCHART]

---

```markdown
| COR | LOE  | Recommendations                                                                                                           |
|-----|------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | A    | In adults with confirmed hypertension who are at increased risk* for CVD, an SBP goal of at least &#x3C;130 mm Hg, with encouragement to achieve SBP &#x3C;120 mm Hg, is recommended to reduce the risk of cardiovascular events and total mortality.1–4 |
| 2b  | B-NR | In adults with confirmed hypertension who are not at increased risk* for CVD, an SBP goal of &#x3C;130 mm Hg, with encouragement to achieve SBP &#x3C;120 mm Hg, may be reasonable to reduce risk of further elevation of BP.⁵ |
| 3   | B-R  | In adults with confirmed hypertension who are at increased risk* for CVD, a DBP target of &#x3C;80 mm Hg is recommended to reduce the risk of cardiovascular events and total mortality.⁶ |
| 4   | B-NR | In adults with confirmed hypertension who are not at increased risk* for CVD, a DBP target of &#x3C;80 mm Hg may be reasonable to reduce the risk of cardiovascular events.⁵ |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Assess patient for hypertension
- Confirm diagnosis of hypertension
- Determine risk for CVD (Increased risk defined as a 10-year predicted risk for CVD events of ≥7.5% using PREVENT)

├── If at increased risk for CVD
│   ├── Set SBP goal: &#x3C;130 mm Hg
│   ├── Encourage achieving SBP &#x3C;120 mm Hg
│   └── Set DBP target: &#x3C;80 mm Hg
│
└── If not at increased risk for CVD
├── Set SBP goal: &#x3C;130 mm Hg
├── Encourage achieving SBP &#x3C;120 mm Hg
└── Set DBP target: &#x3C;80 mm Hg
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Start
2. Assess Blood Pressure
- If High Blood Pressure
- Proceed to Step 3
- If Normal Blood Pressure
- Monitor and Reassess
3. Determine Treatment Options
- If Patient is Not on Medication
- Consider Lifestyle Modifications
- Initiate Antihypertensive Medication
- If Patient is on Medication
- Assess Current Medication Efficacy
- Adjust Dosage or Change Medication
4. Select Medication Class
- ACE Inhibitors
- ARBs
- Calcium Channel Blockers
- Beta Blockers
- Diuretics
5. Determine Combination Therapy if Necessary
- If Single Agent Ineffective
- Combine Medications
6. Monitor Patient Response
- If Blood Pressure Controlled
- Continue Current Regimen
- If Blood Pressure Uncontrolled
- Reassess and Adjust Treatment
7. End
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| Self-management interventions                                                   |                                                                                                                       |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Assessment of Hypertension**
- Check blood pressure levels.
- Determine if the patient is at high risk for CVD.

2. **If not at high risk for CVD:**
- Average DBP between 80-89 mm Hg.
- Treatment with low-dose chlorthalidone (12.5 mg) and amiloride (2.5 mg).
- Monitor for changes in BP and left ventricular mass.

3. **If at high risk for CVD:**
- Randomize to DBP &#x3C;80 mm Hg vs higher DBP treatment goal.
- Monitor CVD risk and all-cause mortality.

4. **Electrolyte Imbalances:**
- Check basic metabolic panel at diagnosis and 2-4 weeks after medication initiation.
- Monitor for hypokalemia, hyperkalemia, and hyponatremia.
- Implement strategies to mitigate disturbances (dietary changes, supplementation).

5. **If severe electrolyte imbalances occur:**
- Discontinue causative medication.
- Treat the imbalance immediately.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| Amlodipine, furosemide, metoprolol,      | Potassium binder (patiromer)               | Decreased absorption of antihypertensives leading to reduced BP-lowering effects. To mitigate this, administer the antihypertensives at least 3 h before or after taking the potassium binder |
| carvedilol, bisoprolol, nebivolol,       |                                             |                                                                                                     |
| telmisartan                               |                                             |                                                                                                     |

|                                           | cyclosilicate)                             |                                                                                                     |
| Methyldopa                                | Iron salts                                  | Decreased absorption of methyldopa leading to reduced BP lowering                                   |
| Bisoprolol, carvedilol, metoprolol       | CYP2D6 inhibitors (eg, amiodarone,        | Increased BB concentration leading to enhanced clinical effects (eg, hypotension and bradycardia)  |
|                                           | cimetidine, diphenhydramine, fluoxetine,  |                                                                                                     |
|                                           | paroxetine, terbinafine)                   |                                                                                                     |
| Diltiazem, verapamil                      | CYP3A4 inhibitors (eg, clarithromycin,    | Increased nondihydropyridine concentration leading to enhanced clinical effects (eg, hypotension and bradycardia) |
|                                           | erythromycin, itraconazole, ketoconazole)  |                                                                                                     |
| Diltiazem, verapamil                      | CYP3A4 inducers (eg, carbamazepine,       | Decreased nondihydropyridine CCB concentration leading to reduced clinical effects (eg, minimization of blood pressure and pulse lowering) |
|                                           | phenobarbital, phenytoin, St. John’s      |                                                                                                     |

| CYP3A4 inhibition via amlodipine,        | Tacrolimus, cyclosporine                   | Increased calcineurin inhibitor concentration leading to increased risk for side effects (eg, renal impairment) |
| verapamil, or diltiazem or other         |                                             |                                                                                                     |
| CYP3A4 inhibitors                         | Dabigatran, rivaroxaban                     | Increased concentration leading to increased risk for bleeding                                       |
|                                           | Atorvastatin, simvastatin                   | Increased statin concentration leading to increased risk for side effects (eg, myopathy)            |
|                                           | Colchicine                                  | Increased colchicine concentration leading to increased risk for adverse effects (eg, neuromuscular toxicity) |
|                                           | Eplerenone                                  | Increased risk of hypotension and hyperkalemia. Using a lower dose of eplerenone when combined with diltiazem could be considered a productive interaction, as the inhibition of eplerenone’s metabolism might allow for lower doses to be effective, reducing the risk of adverse effects while maintaining efficacy |
| Thiazide-type diuretics, RAS blockers     | Lithium                                      | Reduced lithium clearance leading to increased lithium toxicity risk                                 |
| Verapamil via P-gp inhibition             | Dabigatran                                   | Reduced P-gp efflux of dabigatran leading to increased dabigatran levels, which results in a higher risk of bleeding |
| Verapamil and carvedilol via P-gp        | Digoxin                                      | Reduced P-gp efflux of digoxin leading to increased digoxin levels, resulting in a higher risk of digoxin toxicity |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Initiate Antihypertensive Medications**
- Measure estimated GFR using serum creatinine 2 to 4 weeks after initiation or dose titration.
- If new kidney dysfunction/injury is observed:
- Evaluate volume status to rule out hypovolemia and other causes.
- Consider holding or reducing diuretic dose.
- Advance more slowly if necessary.
- If eGFR decline is persistently >30%:
- Referral to nephrologist for evaluation of other causes of AKI, CKD.
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
|                                                       | Venlafaxine                                                                     | Reduced BP lowering                                                                                  |

| Clonidine                                             | Noncardioselective BB (eg, nadolol or propranolol)                            | Unopposed alpha agonism upon BB withdrawal leading to hypertensive crisis                           |

| RAS inhibitors                                        | Diuretics                                                                      | Balanced effects on serum potassium levels with diminished possibility for hypokalemia (with diuretic) or hyperkalemia (with RAASi) |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Assess patient for hypertension.
- Check for target organ damage.

2. **Is the patient diagnosed with T2D?**
- Yes: Proceed to step 3.
- No: Follow general hypertension guidelines.

3. **Is SBP ≥ 130 mm Hg?**
- Yes: Initiate antihypertensive drug treatment.
- No: Monitor and encourage lifestyle changes.

4. **Set treatment goal for SBP.**
- Target: &#x3C;130 mm Hg, encourage &#x3C;120 mm Hg.

5. **Is DBP ≥ 80 mm Hg?**
- Yes: Set treatment goal for DBP &#x3C;80 mm Hg.
- No: Monitor and encourage lifestyle changes.

6. **Select antihypertensive agents.**
- First-line options: Thiazide-type diuretics, long-acting CCB, ACEi, ARB.

7. **Is there CKD present (eGFR &#x3C;60 mL/min/1.73 m² or albuminuria ≥30 mg/g)?**
- Yes: Recommend ACEi or ARB.
- No: Continue with selected antihypertensive agents.

8. **Monitor for target organ damage.**
- Adjust treatment as necessary based on organ health.

9. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Markdown Table

| COR | LOE | Recommendations                                                                                                           |
|-----|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 2b  | B-R | 1. In adults with hypertension who also have overweight or obesity with a BMI ≥27 kg/m², incretin mimetics (eg, GLP-1 receptor agonists) when used for weight management may be effective as an adjunct to lower BP. |
| 2b  | B-R | 2. In adults with hypertension who have obesity with a BMI ≥35.0 kg/m², bariatric surgery (when considered for weight loss) in combination with behavioral interventions and antihypertensive therapies may be effective at lowering BP. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Hypertension in Adults**
- Assess for comorbidities (e.g., T2D, obesity).
- If T2D is present, evaluate cardiovascular risk.
- If moderate to severe albuminuria is present:
- Recommend ACEi or ARB for CVD event reduction.
- If no albuminuria:
- Consider ACEi or ARB for CVD event reduction.
2. **Obesity and Metabolic Syndrome**
- If BMI ≥27 kg/m²:
- Recommend incretin mimetics for weight management.
- If BMI ≥35.0 kg/m²:
- Consider bariatric surgery with behavioral interventions and antihypertensive therapies.
3. **Management of Hypertension**
- Utilize antihypertensive drug classes (ACEi, ARB, CCB, diuretics).
- Monitor for efficacy in reducing urinary albumin excretion.
4. **Lifestyle Interventions**
- Consider intensive lifestyle interventions, pharmacotherapies, and bariatric surgery for weight loss.
- Evaluate the role of time-restricted eating in improving metabolic health and lowering BP.
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Lưu đồ chẩn đoán/điều trị

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá bệnh nhân có huyết áp cao.
- Nếu có bệnh lý mạn tính (Chronic Coronary Disease - CCD):
- **Rẽ nhánh**: Có thừa cân hoặc béo phì và không có tiểu đường?
- Có: Sử dụng GLP-1 receptor agonists để giảm huyết áp.
- Không: Tiếp tục theo dõi và điều trị huyết áp.
- Nếu không có bệnh lý mạn tính:
- **Rẽ nhánh**: Huyết áp tâm thu (SBP) có dưới 130 mm Hg không?
- Có: Tiếp tục theo dõi.
- Không: Điều trị để giảm SBP xuống dưới 130 mm Hg.
2. **Điều trị**: Sử dụng ACEi, ARB, BB, CCB, thiazide-type diuretics, hoặc MRA nếu cần thiết.
3. **Theo dõi**: Đánh giá lại huyết áp và điều chỉnh điều trị nếu cần.
4. **Kết thúc**: Đảm bảo bệnh nhân hiểu về tầm quan trọng của việc kiểm soát huyết áp.
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Markdown Table

| Section | Content |
|---------|---------|
| 5.3.4.2 | HF With Preserved Ejection Fraction Synopsis: Hypertension is a major risk factor for developing heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF) and an important target for HF management to reduce hospitalization, CVD events, and mortality. Appropriate use of diuretics is crucial to the success of other antihypertensive medications in the presence of HFpEF and should be used for signs and/or symptoms of volume overload. RAASi are indicated for management of HFpEF to attain an SBP of &#x3C;130 mm Hg, especially with an MRA or ARNi, or ARB when ARNi is not feasible. BB are not recommended for hypertension management with HFpEF given negative chronotropic effects and should be restricted to specific comorbid conditions (eg, arrhythmia, ACS). SGLT2i are used frequently for HFpEF treatment (with and without diabetes), unless contraindicated, to reduce the risk of hospitalization and cardiovascular mortality. SGLT2i may lower BP; therefore, adjustment in other antihypertensive medications may be indicated if signs or symptoms of hypotension are present. For more information on the management of and guidelines for HFpEF in adults, see the “2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA Guideline for the Management of Heart Failure,” and the “2023 ACC Expert Consensus Decision Pathway on Management of Heart Failure With Preserved Ejection Fraction.” |
| 5.3.4.1 | HF With Reduced Ejection Fraction Synopsis: Hypertension is the most common medical comorbidity in patients with HF, and its prevalence among patients with heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF), defined as left ventricular ejection fraction ≤40%, continues to rise. Hypertension is known to be a major risk factor for HFrEF directly through alterations in cardiac structure and function in response to chronic pressure overload and indirectly through its associations with ischemic heart disease. In patients with HFrEF and hypertension, uptitration of HF GDMT to the maximally tolerated dose is recommended for hypertension control. Clinical trials assessing the impact of BP reduction on outcomes in patients with HFrEF and hypertension are limited, and the optimal BP goal is unknown; however, a goal SBP &#x3C;130 mm Hg should at least be attained in patients with hypertension and HFrEF. Diuretics should be added as needed for volume overload. Dihydropyridine CCB may be used to treat hypertension in patients with elevated BP despite the optimization of GDMT. Nondihydropyridine CCB may be harmful in patients with HFrEF due to their negative inotropic effects and are not recommended for hypertension management. For information on the management of HFrEF in adults, see the guidelines. |
| 5.3.5 | Atrial Fibrillation Synopsis: Hypertension has the highest attributable risk for the development of AF. It is present in >80% of patients with AF and is the most common comorbid condition, regardless of age. Both AF and hypertension increase in frequency with age, and because of the close relationship between BP and AF, hypertension remains a key component in several AF and CVD risk prediction scores. BP control in individuals with hypertension reduces the risk for incident AF, especially in patients with HF. In adults with AF and hypertension, optimal BP control reduces rates of MACE, including stroke. Lifestyle modifications that result in lower BP may decrease the recurrence of AF. Small studies and secondary analyses of RCTs reported lower incident AF with ACEi or ARB, and 2 meta-analyses suggest reduction in recurrent AF with ACEi or ARB, although more definitive evidence is needed. RCTs and observational studies suggest that MRAs reduce AF burden. Control of hypertension is a key component of AF management, although optimal treatment targets for the management of hypertension in AF remain unclear. Therefore, it is reasonable to apply general hypertension guidelines to adults with AF, which would include attaining optimal BP control. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Hypertension Management**
- Assess patient for hypertension.
- Determine if patient has HFpEF or HFrEF.
- If HFpEF:
- Use diuretics for volume overload.
- Initiate RAASi to achieve SBP &#x3C;130 mm Hg.
- Avoid BB unless specific comorbid conditions are present.
- Consider SGLT2i for treatment unless contraindicated.
- If HFrEF:
- Optimize HF GDMT to maximally tolerated dose.
- Aim for SBP &#x3C;130 mm Hg.
- Add diuretics as needed for volume overload.
- Avoid nondihydropyridine CCB.
2. **Atrial Fibrillation Management**
- Assess for presence of AF in hypertensive patients.
- Control BP to reduce risk of incident AF.
- Consider lifestyle modifications to lower BP.
- Use ACEi or ARB if indicated to reduce AF recurrence.
- Monitor and adjust treatment as necessary.
[END_FLOWCHART]

---

```markdown
| Drug Class                          | Notes on Use                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
|-------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| BB                                  | In patients with HFrEF, even if asymptomatic, use 1 of the 3 BBs proven to reduce mortality and hospitalizations (bisoprolol, carvedilol, metoprolol succinate).                                            |
| MRA                                 | In patients with symptomatic HFrEF, spironolactone or eplerenone is recommended to reduce morbidity and mortality if eGFR is >30 mL/min/1.73 m² and potassium is &#x3C;5.0 mEq/L.                             |
| RAASi with ACEi or ARB or ARNi     | In patients with HFrEF and NYHA functional class II to III symptoms, ARNi is recommended to reduce morbidity and mortality. When the use of ARNi is not feasible, ACEi or ARB is recommended to reduce morbidity and mortality. |
| SGLT2i                              | SGLT2i are recommended in patients with symptomatic HFrEF to reduce hospitalization and cardiovascular mortality irrespective of the presence of type 2 diabetes.                                           |
| Hydralazine and isosorbide dinitrate| For patients self-identified as Black with NYHA functional class III to IV HFrEF who are receiving optimal medical therapy, the combination of hydralazine and isosorbide dinitrate is recommended to improve symptoms and reduce morbidity and mortality. In patients with current or previous symptomatic HFrEF who cannot be given first-line agents, such as ARNi, ACEi, or ARB, because of drug intolerance or renal insufficiency, a combination of hydralazine and isosorbide dinitrate might be considered to reduce morbidity and mortality. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Patient with Hypertension and HFrEF**
- Assess symptoms and eGFR
- If asymptomatic, consider BB (bisoprolol, carvedilol, metoprolol succinate)
- If symptomatic and eGFR >30 mL/min/1.73 m² and potassium &#x3C;5.0 mEq/L:
- Use MRA (spironolactone or eplerenone)
- If NYHA functional class II to III:
- Use ARNi
- If ARNi not feasible, use ACEi or ARB
- If symptomatic HFrEF:
- Use SGLT2i
- If patient self-identifies as Black with NYHA functional class III to IV:
- Use hydralazine and isosorbide dinitrate
- If intolerant to first-line agents:
- Consider hydralazine and isosorbide dinitrate
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| **Clinical Statements**                                                                 | **Guidelines**                                                                                       |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| The prevalence of hypertension is 67% to 92% among people with CKD.                   | CKD is an important risk factor for CVD, and the coexistence of hypertension and CKD increases the risk of CVD events. |
| A majority of people with CKD have uncontrolled BP.                                   | Intensive BP treatment (mean achieved SBP 121 mm Hg) reduced the risk of CVD compared to standard treatment (mean achieved SBP 136 mm Hg). |
| Treatment goal SBP &#x3C;130 mm Hg balances benefits of intensive BP lowering with risks of adverse events. | An ACEi or an ARB is recommended for initial treatment of hypertension in CKD due to long-term kidney and CVD benefits. |
| ACEi or ARB may be considered for those with lower level albuminuria (&#x3C;30 mg/g).      | Recommendations refer to people with nondialysis-requiring CKD.                                   |
| ACEi or ARB is appropriate for less-severe CKD (stage 1 or 2) with moderate or severe albuminuria. | Immunosuppression may contribute to the risk of hypertension in organ transplant recipients.         |
| Calcineurin inhibitor-based immunosuppression regimens are associated with a high prevalence of hypertension. | There are no robust trials in post-transplant patients comparing different BP targets or drug choices. |
| A systematic review did not find that any BP-lowering medication class reduced the risk of graft loss. | Insufficient evidence to support specific recommendations on BP targets or recommended agents for kidney transplant recipients. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Hypertension in CKD**
- Prevalence: 67% to 92%
- Risk Factor: Increases CVD risk

2. **Initial Treatment**
- Recommendation: ACEi or ARB
- Consideration: Moderate or severe albuminuria (≥30 mg/g)

3. **Monitoring**
- Recheck BP: 2 to 4 weeks after initiating or intensifying ACEi or ARB dosage
- Monitor for: Hyperkalemia or decline in eGFR >30%

4. **Treatment Goal**
- Target SBP: &#x3C;130 mm Hg
- Balance: Benefits of intensive BP lowering vs. risks of adverse events

5. **Post-Transplant Considerations**
- Hypertension common due to pre-existing conditions and immunosuppressive medications
- High prevalence of masked hypertension in transplant recipients

6. **Evidence Review**
- Insufficient evidence for specific BP targets in transplant recipients
- No robust trials comparing different BP targets or drug choices

7. **Cerebrovascular Disease**
- Stroke management: Complex due to heterogeneous causes
- Treatment recommendations: Consider stroke acuity, type, and therapeutic objectives
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| COR | LOE  | Recommendations                                                                                                           |
|-----|------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 2a  | A    | For adult patients with acute spontaneous intracerebral hemorrhage (ICH) who present with SBP between 150 and 220 mm Hg, it can be beneficial to immediately lower SBP to 130 to &#x3C;140 mm Hg for at least 7 days after ICH to improve functional outcomes but stop antihypertensive medications if SBP &#x3C;130 mm Hg. |
| 2a  | B-NR | In adults with acute spontaneous ICH requiring acute BP lowering, careful titration to ensure smooth, nonlabile, and sustained control of BP, avoiding peaks and large variability in SBP, can be beneficial for improving functional outcomes. |
| 3: Harm | B-NR | For adult patients with acute spontaneous ICH who present with SBP >220 mm Hg, SBP should not be lowered below 130 mm Hg to reduce adverse events. |
| 1   | C-LD | In patients with acute ischemic stroke, hypotension and hypovolemia should be corrected to maintain systemic perfusion levels necessary to support organ function. |
| 1   | B-NR | Patients who have elevated BP and are otherwise eligible for treatment with IV thrombolytics should have their BP lowered to SBP &#x3C;185 mm Hg and DBP &#x3C;110 mm Hg before IV thrombolytic therapy is initiated and should be maintained below 180/105 mm Hg for at least the first 24 hours after initiating thrombolytic therapy to avoid complications. |
| 2a  | B-NR | In patients who undergo endovascular treatment, it is reasonable to maintain the BP at ≤180/105 mm Hg during and for 24 hours after the procedure to improve long-term functional outcomes and prevent death. |
| 2b  | C-LD | In patients with BP of ≥220/120 mm Hg who did not receive IV thrombolytic or endovascular treatment and have no comorbid conditions requiring acute antihypertensive treatment, it might be reasonable to lower BP by 15% during the first 24 hours after onset of stroke to improve outcomes. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Acute Intracerebral Hemorrhage (ICH) Management**
- Presenting SBP between 150 and 220 mm Hg
- **Action**: Lower SBP to 130 to &#x3C;140 mm Hg for at least 7 days
- If SBP &#x3C;130 mm Hg, **Stop antihypertensive medications**
- Presenting SBP >220 mm Hg
- **Action**: Do not lower SBP below 130 mm Hg
- Monitor for adverse events
- Requiring acute BP lowering
- **Action**: Careful titration to ensure smooth and sustained BP control
- Avoid peaks and large variability in SBP
[END_FLOWCHART]

[START_FLOWCHART]
2. **Acute Ischemic Stroke Management**
- Hypotension and hypovolemia present
- **Action**: Correct to maintain systemic perfusion levels
- Elevated BP and eligible for IV thrombolytics
- **Action**: Lower BP to SBP &#x3C;185 mm Hg and DBP &#x3C;110 mm Hg before therapy
- Maintain BP below 180/105 mm Hg for 24 hours post-therapy
- Undergoing endovascular treatment
- **Action**: Maintain BP at ≤180/105 mm Hg during and for 24 hours after procedure
- BP ≥220/120 mm Hg without IV thrombolytic or endovascular treatment
- **Action**: Consider lowering BP by 15% during the first 24 hours
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

```markdown
| COR          | LOE          | Recommendations                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
|--------------|--------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 5            | 3: No Benefit | In patients with BP &#x3C;220/120 mm Hg who do not receive IV thrombolysis or endovascular treatment and do not have a comorbid condition requiring urgent antihypertensive treatment, initiating or reinitiating treatment of hypertension within the first 48 to 72 hours after an acute ischemic stroke is not effective to prevent death or disability. |
| 6            | 3: Harm      | In patients undergoing successful brain reperfusion with endovascular treatment for a large vessel occlusion, lowering SBP &#x3C;140 mm Hg within the first 24 to 72 hours after reperfusion can worsen long-term functional outcome. |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Patient presents with acute ischemic stroke**
- Assess blood pressure (BP)
- If BP &#x3C;220/120 mm Hg:
- If patient receives IV thrombolysis or endovascular treatment:
- Monitor BP
- If BP >180 mm Hg:
- Consider treatment
- If BP &#x3C;180 mm Hg:
- No treatment needed
- If patient does not receive IV thrombolysis or endovascular treatment:
- Initiate or reinitiate antihypertensive treatment within 48 to 72 hours
- Monitor for effectiveness
- If BP >220/120 mm Hg:
- Assess for comorbid conditions
- If comorbid conditions present:
- Initiate treatment
- If no comorbid conditions:
- Consider risks of rapid BP reduction
- Individualize treatment approach
2. **Post-endovascular treatment**
- Monitor BP
- If SBP &#x3C;140 mm Hg:
- Assess for potential harm
- If SBP >140 mm Hg:
- Consider treatment to lower BP
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

| COR | LOE | Recommendations                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |

| 1   | A   | In patients with hypertension who have experienced an ischemic stroke, transient ischemic attack (TIA), or ICH, treatment with a thiazide-type diuretic, ACEi, or ARB is recommended for lowering BP and reducing recurrent stroke and ICH risk.1–3 |
| 1   | B-R | In patients with hypertension who have experienced an ischemic stroke, TIA, or ICH, an office SBP/DBP goal of &#x3C;130/80 mm Hg is recommended to reduce the risk of recurrent stroke, ICH, and other vascular events.1,3–5                |
| 2a  | B-R | In patients with no history of hypertension who have experienced an ischemic stroke, TIA, or ICH and have an average office SBP/DBP of ≥130/80 mm Hg, antihypertensive medication treatment can be beneficial to reduce the risk of recurrent stroke, ICH, and other vascular events.5–7 |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Patient with Hypertension**
- Experienced ischemic stroke, TIA, or ICH
- **Action**: Recommend thiazide-type diuretic, ACEi, or ARB
- **Outcome**: Lower BP and reduce recurrent stroke and ICH risk

2. **Patient with Hypertension**
- Experienced ischemic stroke, TIA, or ICH
- **Action**: Set office SBP/DBP goal of &#x3C;130/80 mm Hg
- **Outcome**: Reduce risk of recurrent stroke, ICH, and other vascular events

3. **Patient without History of Hypertension**
- Experienced ischemic stroke, TIA, or ICH
- Average office SBP/DBP of ≥130/80 mm Hg
- **Action**: Consider antihypertensive medication treatment
- **Outcome**: Reduce risk of recurrent stroke, ICH, and other vascular events
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Sơ đồ/Lưu đồ

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Bắt đầu**: Đánh giá bệnh nhân có huyết áp cao.
- Nếu có, tiếp tục đến bước 2.
- Nếu không, kết thúc.

2. **Đánh giá huyết áp tâm thu (SBP)**:
- Nếu SBP &#x3C; 130 mm Hg, khuyến nghị điều trị để ngăn ngừa suy giảm nhận thức nhẹ và chứng mất trí nhớ.
- Nếu SBP ≥ 130 mm Hg, tiếp tục đến bước 3.

3. **Xem xét các yếu tố nguy cơ khác**:
- Nếu có yếu tố nguy cơ cho bệnh mạch máu não, khuyến nghị điều trị huyết áp.
- Nếu không, theo dõi định kỳ.

4. **Theo dõi và điều trị**:
- Đặt mục tiêu huyết áp &#x3C; 130/80 mm Hg.
- Đánh giá hiệu quả điều trị sau 3.5 năm.

5. **Kết quả**:
- Nếu có sự cải thiện trong nhận thức, tiếp tục theo dõi.
- Nếu không, xem xét các phương pháp điều trị khác.

[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
| 1   | A    | For adults with uncontrolled hypertension, a team-based care approach is recommended to achieve and maintain BP control.1–4                                                                                                       |
| 1   | C-LD | For adults with uncontrolled hypertension, an evidence-based care plan utilizing HBPM, and team-based care that is responsive to addressing adverse SDOH, is recommended to achieve and maintain BP control.5,6                     |
| 1   | B-NR | For adults with uncontrolled hypertension, an integrated treatment model that includes accurate BP measurement, prompt treatment, patient engagement, and ongoing review of HBPM is recommended to improve BP control.7–10              |
| 1   | B-R  | Adults with uncontrolled hypertension placed on new or intensified medical therapy should have follow-up evaluations for medication adherence and response to treatment at monthly intervals until control is achieved.11–13            |
| 1   | B-R  | For adults with uncontrolled hypertension, health information technology (HIT) by synchronous (eg, phone, video call) or asynchronous (eg, text, e-mail) communication is beneficial in improving BP control, access to care, and adherence to standards of care. |
| 1   | B-NR | In adults with undiagnosed or uncontrolled hypertension, use of the electronic health record (EHR) and patient registries is beneficial for screening and identification of hypertension to focus on those who need additional care.¹⁵ |
| 2a  | B-R  | In adults with uncontrolled hypertension, telehealth interventions can be useful to reduce BP18–26 and improve office BP control.19,21,23–26                                                                                       |
```

```markdown
| Responsibilities and Roles of the Hypertension Team | Description                                                                                          |
|-----------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Communication, shared decision-making, and care coordination among various clinical team members, the patient, and patient caregivers |                                                                                                      |
| Effective use of evidence-based diagnosis and management guidelines |                                                                                                      |
| Regular, structured follow-up mechanisms and reminder systems to monitor patient progress |                                                                                                      |
| Medication adherence support and patient education about hypertension medication |                                                                                                      |
| Medication initiation, addition, and titration using evidence-based treatment algorithms |                                                                                                      |
| Use of evidence-based tools and resources designed to maximize self-management (including health behavior change, lifestyle modification, etc) |                                                                                                      |

| Individual Hypertension Team Members | Roles (examples)                                                                                     |
|--------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Primary care physician/cardiologist, physician assistant or associate/nurse practitioner/advanced practice nurse | Routine and complex hypertension care, managing primary care issues                                   |
| Cardiologist/physician assistant or associate/nurse practitioner/advanced practice nurse | Routine and complex hypertension care, especially for patients with cardiac disease or high risk for major cardiovascular events |
| Nephrologist, endocrinologist, hypertension specialist | Management of complex hypertension care, especially due to secondary causes, and/or resistant hypertension |
| Nurse (including in-office, home care, internal and external population health personnel) | Accurate assessment of BP, medication reconciliation, patient education, self-management, lifestyle modification, and adherence |
| Clinical Pharmacist | Comprehensive medication management, identification of medication-related interactions, and educating patients on their medication regimen |
| Dietician | Ongoing patient-centered counseling to assess dietary habits and preferences and set and monitor goals for healthy lifestyle |
| Social Worker | Assess for psychosocial, cultural, and financial barriers and find solutions to overcome these barriers |
| Community health worker | Assess and address social determinants of health and identify and promote acceptable community-based resources to overcome these barriers |
```

```markdown
2. Initiate Pharmacotherapy

- Monitor BP response and medication adherence
```

### Markdown Table

| COR | LOE | Recommendations                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
|-----|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | A   | For individuals with hypertension who are planning a pregnancy or who become pregnant, labetalol and extended-release nifedipine are preferred agents to treat hypertension and minimize fetal risk.                                 |
| 1   | B-R | Individuals with hypertension who are planning a pregnancy or who become pregnant should be counseled about the benefits of low-dose (81 mg/day) aspirin to reduce the risk of preeclampsia and its sequelae.                       |
| 1   | B-R | Pregnant individuals with SBP ≥160 mm Hg or DBP ≥110 mm Hg confirmed on repeat measurement within 15 minutes should receive antihypertensive medication (Table 23) to lower BP to &#x3C;160/&#x3C;110 mm Hg within 30 to 60 minutes.      |
| 1   | B-R | Pregnant individuals with chronic hypertension (defined as prepregnancy hypertension or SBP 140 to 159 mm Hg and/or DBP 90 to 109 mm Hg prior to 20 weeks’ gestation) should receive antihypertensive therapy to achieve BP &#x3C;140/90 mm Hg. |
| 3: Harm | C-LD | Individuals with hypertension who are planning a pregnancy or who become pregnant should not be treated with atenolol, ACEi, ARB, direct renin inhibitors, nitroprusside, or MRA to avoid fetal harm.                             |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Hypertension Management in Pregnancy**
- **Step 1:** Identify individuals with hypertension planning or becoming pregnant.
- **Action:** Counsel on preferred medications (labetalol, extended-release nifedipine).
- **Step 2:** Assess blood pressure (BP) levels.
- **If SBP ≥160 mm Hg or DBP ≥110 mm Hg:**
- **Action:** Administer antihypertensive medication to lower BP to &#x3C;160/&#x3C;110 mm Hg within 30 to 60 minutes.
- **If chronic hypertension (SBP 140-159 mm Hg or DBP 90-109 mm Hg before 20 weeks):**
- **Action:** Initiate antihypertensive therapy to achieve BP &#x3C;140/90 mm Hg.
- **Step 3:** Provide counseling on low-dose aspirin (81 mg/day) to reduce preeclampsia risk.
- **Step 4:** Avoid certain medications (atenolol, ACEi, ARB, direct renin inhibitors, nitroprusside, MRA) to prevent fetal harm.
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Markdown Table

| Clinical Statements and Guidelines                                                                                     |
|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| The management of BP in pregnancy-capable individuals requires special considerations.                                 |
| The overarching goals of antihypertensive treatment during pregnancy are aimed at preventing severe hypertension and preeclampsia and optimizing maternal and fetal/neonatal clinical outcomes. |
| Compared with the diagnostic criteria for hypertension in adults, ACOG defines hypertension in pregnancy as an SBP ≥140 mm Hg or DBP ≥90 mm Hg on 2 occasions at least 4 hours apart. |
| Severe hypertension in pregnancy is defined as SBP ≥160 mm Hg or DBP ≥110 mm Hg. When left untreated, it can result in maternal stroke, renal insufficiency or kidney failure, MI, HF, placental abruption, preterm birth, fetal growth restriction, and maternal death from intracerebral hemorrhage and/or stillbirth or perinatal death. |
| Pregnant individuals with elevated BP are classified as having 1 of the HDP based upon gestational age at diagnosis and the presence of target organ involvement. |
| Vascular and hemodynamic alterations in pregnancy result in a decline of BP by 10% in early pregnancy, reaching a nadir in the second trimester and slowly rising back to baseline by the end of the third trimester. |
| Immediate-release oral nifedipine has been shown in a meta-analysis to be associated with faster time to achieve target BP specifically in pregnancy. |
| A Cochrane review and meta-analysis of 58 trials concluded that the use of antihypertensive medications reduced the risk of severe-range hypertension but did not significantly reduce the risk of preeclampsia. |
| Chronic hypertension is associated with a high risk of developing preeclampsia, a multiorgan system inflammatory syndrome. |
| Daily low-dose aspirin taken during pregnancy after 12 weeks’ gestation has been shown to significantly reduce the risk for preeclampsia in individuals at moderate or high risk compared with placebo. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Management of BP in Pregnancy-Capable Individuals**
- Goal: Prevent severe hypertension and preeclampsia.
- Optimize maternal and fetal/neonatal outcomes.

2. **Diagnosis of Hypertension**
- ACOG Criteria:
- SBP ≥140 mm Hg or DBP ≥90 mm Hg on 2 occasions at least 4 hours apart.
- Severe Hypertension:
- SBP ≥160 mm Hg or DBP ≥110 mm Hg.

3. **Consequences of Untreated Severe Hypertension**
- Maternal stroke
- Renal insufficiency or kidney failure
- Myocardial infarction (MI)
- Heart failure (HF)
- Placental abruption
- Preterm birth
- Fetal growth restriction
- Maternal death from intracerebral hemorrhage or stillbirth/perinatal death.

4. **Classification of Hypertension in Pregnancy**
- Based on gestational age at diagnosis and target organ involvement.

5. **Vascular and Hemodynamic Changes**
- BP declines by 10% in early pregnancy, nadir in second trimester, rises to baseline by third trimester.

6. **Treatment Options**
- Immediate-release oral nifedipine for faster BP control.
- Antihypertensive medications reduce risk of severe-range hypertension but not preeclampsia.

7. **Chronic Hypertension Management**
- High risk of preeclampsia.
- Daily low-dose aspirin after 12 weeks reduces risk for preeclampsia in moderate/high-risk individuals.

[END_FLOWCHART]

---

```markdown
| Condition                                   | Definition                                                                                          |
|---------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Chronic hypertension                        | Diagnosis prior to pregnancy or at &#x3C;20 wks’ gestation                                              |
| Gestational hypertension                     | De novo hypertension at ≥20 wks’ gestation in the absence of proteinuria or other signs of pre-eclampsia |
| Preeclampsia                                | Gestational hypertension with proteinuria or other maternal end-organ dysfunction                   |
| Preeclampsia superimposed on chronic hypertension | Preeclampsia in a woman with a history of hypertension before pregnancy or before 20 weeks’ gestation |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Assess for hypertension in pregnancy.

2. **Is the diagnosis prior to pregnancy or at &#x3C;20 weeks’ gestation?**
- Yes: Chronic hypertension
- No: Proceed to next step.

3. **Is the hypertension de novo at ≥20 weeks’ gestation?**
- Yes: Assess for proteinuria or other signs of pre-eclampsia.
- No: End assessment.

4. **Is there proteinuria or other maternal end-organ dysfunction?**
- Yes: Preeclampsia
- No: Gestational hypertension

5. **Does the woman have a history of hypertension before pregnancy or before 20 weeks’ gestation?**
- Yes: Preeclampsia superimposed on chronic hypertension
- No: Proceed to management of gestational hypertension.

6. **Management of gestational hypertension:**
- Monitor blood pressure and assess for severe-range hypertension.
- If severe-range hypertension (SBP ≥160 mm Hg or DBP ≥110 mm Hg), manage similarly to preeclampsia.

7. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
|                         | Commonly initiated at 100-200 mg twice daily.     |                                                                                    |                                    |
| Nifedipine              | 30-120 mg/d orally of an extended-release preparation. | Do not use sublingual form.                                                      | Immediate-release formulation should generally be reserved for control of severe, acutely elevated blood pressures in hospitalized patients. Should be avoided in tachycardia. |
|                         | Commonly initiated at 30-60 mg once daily (extended release). |                                                                                    |                                    |
| Methyldopa              | 500-3000 mg/d orally in 2 to 4 divided doses.    | Safety data up to 7 y of age in offspring.                                       | May not be as effective as other medications, especially in control of severe hypertension. Use limited by side effect profile (sedation, depression, dizziness). |
|                         | Commonly initiated at 250 mg 2 or 3 times daily.  |                                                                                    |                                    |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Initial Assessment of Hypertension in Pregnancy**
- Measure Blood Pressure (BP)
- Determine if BP is elevated

2. **If BP is Elevated**
- Assess for symptoms of preeclampsia
- If symptoms present, proceed to further evaluation
- If no symptoms, monitor BP and consider lifestyle modifications

3. **If Symptoms of Preeclampsia are Present**
- Confirm diagnosis of preeclampsia
- Initiate treatment protocol for preeclampsia

4. **If No Symptoms of Preeclampsia**
- Consider antihypertensive therapy
- Choose appropriate medication based on clinical guidelines
- Monitor response to treatment

5. **Postpartum Follow-Up**
- Schedule BP check within 3 to 10 days of discharge
- Encourage annual BP measurements for individuals with a history of hypertension during pregnancy
- Discuss contraception and low-dose aspirin for future pregnancies if indicated
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

```markdown
| Clinical Statements | Recommendations for Resistant Hypertension and Renal Denervation | COR | LOE |
|---------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
| Blood pressure      | SBP ≥140 mm Hg or DBP ≥90 mm Hg on 2 occasions at least 4 h apart after 20 wks of gestation in a woman with previously normal BP or SBP ≥160 mm Hg or DBP ≥110 mm Hg (severe hypertension can be confirmed within a short interval [min] to facilitate timely antihypertensive therapy). |     |     |
|                     | 5. All patients with hypertension who are being considered for RDN should be evaluated by a multidisciplinary team with expertise in resistant hypertension and RDN. | 1   | B-NR |
|                     | 6. For patients with hypertension for whom RDN is contemplated, the benefits of lowering BP and potential procedural risks compared with continuing medical therapy should be discussed as part of a shared decision-making process to ensure patients choose the therapy that meets their expectations. | 1   | C-EO |
| Proteinuria         | ≥300 mg per 24-h urine collection (or this amount extrapolated from a timed collection) or Protein/creatinine ratio ≥0.3 or Dipstick reading of 2+ (used only if other quantitative methods are not available). |     |     |
| OR in the Absence of Proteinuria, New Onset Hypertension With the New Onset of Any of the Following: | | | |
| Thrombocytopenia    | Platelet count &#x3C;100 × 10⁹/L                                      |     |     |
| Renal insufficiency  | Serum creatinine concentrations >1.1 mg/dL or a doubling of serum creatinine concentration in the absence of other renal disease |     |     |
| Impaired liver function | Elevated blood concentration of liver transaminases to twice normal concentration |     |     |
| Pulmonary edema     |                                                                  |     |     |
| New-onset headache unresponsive to medication and not accounted for by alternative diagnoses or visual symptoms | | | |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Evaluate for Resistant Hypertension
- Confirm BP above goal despite treatment with 3 antihypertensive medications
- Exclude pseudoresistance
- Inaccurate BP measurement
- Interfering medications
- White-coat hypertension
- Nonadherence to medication
2. If uncontrolled resistant hypertension despite optimal treatment:
- Add MRA (Mineralocorticoid Receptor Antagonist)
3. If MRA is not tolerated or contraindicated:
- Consider adding other agents (amiloride, BBs, alpha-blockers, etc.)
4. If still uncontrolled:
- Evaluate for secondary causes of hypertension
5. If resistant hypertension persists:
- Consider Renal Denervation (RDN) as adjunct treatment
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

---

### Markdown Table

| Clinical Statements and Guidelines                                                                                     |
|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1. Approximately 20% of adults with hypertension reported regular use of over-the-counter or nonprescription medications that may directly raise BP or interfere with antihypertensive drug efficacy, such as NSAIDs or nasal decongestants. These medications are associated with uncontrolled BP and should be reviewed during evaluation of patients with resistant hypertension. Some prescription drugs are known to elevate BP and should be replaced with alternative agents that avoid hypertensive side effects, if possible; however, in some settings, such as the treatment of malignant diseases, the contributing medication should be continued if hypertension can be controlled. Secondary hypertension is more common among adults with resistant hypertension, particularly primary aldosteronism, OSA, renal parenchymal disease, and renovascular disease. Screening for secondary hypertension is discussed in Section 3.2.3 (“Secondary Forms of Hypertension”). |
| 2. Antihypertensive drug therapy should start with a combination of an ACEi or ARB, a CCB, and a diuretic. Replacing thiazide-type diuretics (eg, HCTZ or bendroflumethiazide) with thiazide-like diuretics (eg, chlorthalidone and indapamide) may offer additional BP reduction and cardiovascular protection among patients with previous MI or stroke. RCTs have shown that addition of spironolactone (25-50 mg/day) as the fourth drug reduced home and 24-hour SBP by 6.6 to 8.7 mm Hg when compared with placebo in patients with resistant hypertension and eGFR ≥45 mL/min/1.73 m². The reduction in BP was greater than with addition of doxazosin or bisoprolol. The magnitude of reduction in 24-hour systolic and diastolic BP was greater with spironolactone than clonidine in a separate clinical trial. Nevertheless, 4% to 40% of adults with resistant hypertension cannot tolerate spironolactone due to hyperkalemia or antiandrogenic side effects. Eplerenone, a more selective steroidal MRA that avoids the antiandrogenic side effects but may cause hyperkalemia, is a potential alternative to spironolactone, but RCTs have not demonstrated reduction of home BP or 24-hour BP at doses. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start Antihypertensive Therapy**
- Begin with a combination of:
- ACEi or ARB
- CCB
- Diuretic
2. **Evaluate Patient for Secondary Hypertension**
- If secondary hypertension is suspected, conduct screening.
3. **Assess Tolerance to Medications**
- If spironolactone is tolerated:
- Add spironolactone (25-50 mg/day) as the fourth drug.
- If spironolactone is not tolerated:
- Consider eplerenone or alternative agents.
4. **Monitor Blood Pressure Response**
- If BP is controlled:
- Continue current regimen.
- If BP is not controlled:
- Consider adding alternative fourth- and fifth-line therapies (e.g., BBs, alpha blockers, central sympatholytic drugs, direct vasodilators).
5. **Consider Renal Denervation (RDN)**
- If patients have resistant hypertension and intolerable side effects to optimal antihypertensive regimens, consider RDN.
- Ensure patient selection aligns with clinical trial criteria to maximize outcomes.
[END_FLOWCHART]

```markdown
| Exclude pseudoresistance                                                             |                                                                                                |
| - Ensure accurate office BP measurements                                             |                                                                                                |
| - Assess for medication nonadherence with prescribed regimen                        |                                                                                                |
| - Obtain home, work, or ambulatory BP readings to exclude white-coat effect        |                                                                                                |
| Identify and reverse contributing lifestyle factors*                                 |                                                                                                |
| Discontinue or minimize interfering substances                                        |                                                                                                |
| Screen for secondary causes of hypertension‡                                         |                                                                                                |
| Pharmacological treatment                                                             |                                                                                                |
| - Maximize diuretic therapy                                                          |                                                                                                |
|   - Replace thiazide-type diuretics with chlorthalidone 12.5-25 mg qd or indapamide 1.25-2.5 mg qd |                                                                                      |
| - Add spironolactone (25-50 mg qd) or equivalent dosage of eplerenone (25-50 mg BID) if eGFR ≥45 |                                                                                      |
| - Use chlorthalidone or loop diuretics in patients with CKD stage 4 or greater     |                                                                                                |
| - Add agents with different MOA                                                      |                                                                                                |
|   - BB, central sympatholytic drugs, or nondihydropyridine CCB for elevated heart rate |                                                                                      |
|   - Add dual endothelin-receptor antagonist (ERA) or potent vasodilators           |                                                                                                |
|     - Dual ERA, eg, aprocitentan, or direct vasodilator eg, hydralazine or minoxidil (only if already on BB or bradycardic and loop diuretic) |                                                                                      |
| Refer to specialist:                                                                 |                                                                                                |
| - For known or suspected secondary cause(s) of hypertension                        |                                                                                                |
```

```markdown
[START_FLOWCHART]
1. Confirm treatment resistance
- Office BP ≥130/80 mm Hg and on ≥3 antihypertensives
- Combination of ACEi or ARB + CCB + thiazide-like diuretics preferred
- Office BP &#x3C;130/80 mm Hg but requires ≥4 antihypertensives
- Combination of ACEi or ARB + CCB + thiazide-like diuretics preferred
2. Exclude pseudoresistance
- Ensure accurate office BP measurements
- Assess for medication nonadherence with prescribed regimen
- Obtain home, work, or ambulatory BP readings to exclude white-coat effect
3. Identify and reverse contributing lifestyle factors
4. Discontinue or minimize interfering substances
5. Screen for secondary causes of hypertension
6. Pharmacological treatment
- Maximize diuretic therapy
- Replace thiazide-type diuretics with chlorthalidone 12.5-25 mg qd or indapamide 1.25-2.5 mg qd
- Add spironolactone (25-50 mg qd) or equivalent dosage of eplerenone (25-50 mg BID) if eGFR ≥45
- Use chlorthalidone or loop diuretics in patients with CKD stage 4 or greater
- Add agents with different MOA
- BB, central sympatholytic drugs, or nondihydropyridine CCB for elevated heart rate
- Add dual endothelin-receptor antagonist (ERA) or potent vasodilators
- Dual ERA, eg, aprocitentan, or direct vasodilator eg, hydralazine or minoxidil (only if already on BB or bradycardic and loop diuretic)
7. Refer to specialist
- For known or suspected secondary cause(s) of hypertension
- If BP remains uncontrolled >6 months of treatment
[END_FLOWCHART]
```

### Markdown Table

| **Clinical Statements**                                                                 | **Guidelines**                                                                                                           |
|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Resistant hypertension OR uncontrolled hypertension*                                    | • Patients with stage 2 hypertension (with both office SBP ≥140 mm Hg and office DBP ≥90 mm Hg) in whom BP is not at goal despite taking ≥4 antihypertensive medications at optimal dosages (ACEi/ARB + CCB + thiazide-type diuretics, and MRA)38,47 |
|                                                                                         | • Patients with stage 2 hypertension (with both office SBP ≥140 mm Hg and office DBP ≥90 mm Hg) who are unable to take antihypertensive medications at the optimal dosages or additional medications11,12,14 |
| **Contraindications**                                                                   | • Neurogenic orthostatic hypotension                                                                                   |
|                                                                                         | • Pregnancy                                                                                                            |
|                                                                                         | • Fibromuscular dysplasia                                                                                              |
|                                                                                         | • Stented renal artery                                                                                                  |
|                                                                                         | • Renal artery aneurysm                                                                                                |
|                                                                                         | • Significant renal artery stenosis                                                                                     |
|                                                                                         | • Known kidney or secreting adrenal tumors                                                                              |
|                                                                                         | *After evaluation by a multidisciplinary team to screen for secondary hypertension and contraindications and following a shared decision-making process.⁴⁹ |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Patient Evaluation**
- Measure 24-hour ambulatory BP
- Assess orthostatic vital signs
- Screen for secondary hypertension

2. **Presence of Renal Artery Disease?**
- Yes: **Contraindication to RDN**
- No: Proceed to next step

3. **Patient with Stage 2 Hypertension?**
- Yes:
- Are they on ≥4 antihypertensive medications at optimal dosages?
- Yes: **Consider RDN**
- No: **Consider alternative treatments**
- No: **Consider alternative treatments**

4. **Post-RDN Surveillance**
- Monitor for renal artery stenosis or dissection using noninvasive imaging studies (e.g., duplex Doppler, CT angiogram, or MRI angiography)

5. **Shared Decision-Making**
- Discuss procedural risks and potential cardiovascular benefits with the patient
- Ensure patient expectations are met
[END_FLOWCHART]

### Markdown Table

| COR | LOE  | Recommendations                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
|-----|------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1   | C-LD | For adults with a hypertensive emergency related to a compelling condition (e.g., acute aortic syndrome or acute aortic dissection), SBP should be reduced to &#x3C;140 mm Hg for most conditions and to &#x3C;120 mm Hg in aortic dissection during the first hour, while monitoring for other target organ dysfunction. |
| 1   | C-LD | For adults with a hypertensive emergency but without a compelling condition, SBP should be reduced with oral or parenteral therapy by no more than 25% within the first hour; then, if stable, to &#x3C;160/100 mm Hg within the next 2 to 6 hours; and then cautiously to 130 to 140 mm Hg during the next 24 to 48 hours to limit target organ injury. |
| 3   | Harm B-NR | For adults with severe hypertension (>180/120 mm Hg) who are hospitalized for noncardiac conditions without evidence of acute target organ damage, intermittent use of additional IV or oral antihypertensive medications are not recommended to acutely reduce BP. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Assessment for Hypertensive Emergency**
- Is BP >180/120 mm Hg?
- Yes: Proceed to step 2.
- No: Not a hypertensive emergency.

2. **Determine Evidence of Acute Target Organ Damage**
- Is there evidence of acute target organ damage?
- Yes: Proceed to step 3.
- No: Proceed to step 4.

3. **Management of Hypertensive Emergency with Acute Target Organ Damage**
- Admit to intensive care unit for continuous monitoring.
- Consider parenteral administration of appropriate therapy.

4. **Management of Hypertensive Emergency without Acute Target Organ Damage**
- Reduce SBP by no more than 25% within the first hour.
- If stable, reduce to &#x3C;160/100 mm Hg within the next 2 to 6 hours.
- Cautiously reduce to 130 to 140 mm Hg during the next 24 to 48 hours.

5. **Follow-Up**
- Reinstitution or intensification of oral antihypertensive medications is recommended in the outpatient setting for patients without acute target organ damage.
[END_FLOWCHART]

---

### Markdown Table

| **Clinical Statements and Guidelines**                                                                                          |
|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Antihypertensive drug class provides more benefit than harm in hypertensive emergencies.                                      |
| Lack of high-quality RCT evidence is related to small trial sizes, lack of double-blinding, lack of long-term follow-up, and failure to report outcomes. |
| Therapeutic goal: minimize target organ damage safely by rapid recognition and early initiation of appropriate antihypertensive treatment. |
| Strategies to reduce BP should be conservative: 25% reduction in the first hour, followed by gradual further reduction within 24 to 48 hours. |
| Antihypertensive drug treatment should be used with caution in hospitalized patients with asymptomatic severe hypertension due to spontaneous BP falls. |
| Observational studies show initiation or intermittent use of additional IV or oral antihypertensive medications in noncardiac conditions increases risk of in-hospital mortality, AKI, and prolonged hospital stay. |
| Asymptomatic patients with severe hypertension can be treated with careful monitoring using standing medications and avoiding as-needed medications. |
| Treatment of hypertensive emergencies requires rapid recognition and knowledge of unique treatment approaches for causal conditions. |
| Current guideline endorses SBP target to &#x3C;130 mm Hg in most patients with AD and &#x3C;120 mm Hg in selected high-risk patients. |
| Pheochromocytoma hypertensive crisis is a medical emergency requiring prompt BP lowering, but immediate reduction to &#x3C;120 or 140 mm Hg is not always necessary. |
| Clinical trials show increased risk of adverse kidney events with early intensive lowering of SBP to 110 to 139 mm Hg, especially in patients with SBP >220 mm Hg. |
| An attempt should be made to reduce SBP to &#x3C;120 mm Hg in aortic dissection during the first hour and &#x3C;140 mm Hg for most other conditions while monitoring target organ function. |
| There is no RCT evidence for treating other forms of hypertensive emergency without compelling indication. |
| Small clinical trials indicate that diuretics and BBs (except nebivolol) are associated with erectile dysfunction in men, while BBs worsen sexual function in women. |
| ARBs have the most favorable profile in men and women regarding sexual dysfunction. |

### Flowchart

[START_FLOWCHART]
1. **Start**
- Identify hypertensive emergency.
2. **Assess Patient**
- Is the patient symptomatic?
- Yes: Proceed to rapid BP control.
- No: Monitor closely.
3. **Rapid BP Control**
- Initiate antihypertensive treatment.
- Choose agent based on pharmacology and comorbidities.
4. **Monitor BP**
- Is BP > 25% reduction achieved in the first hour?
- Yes: Gradual further reduction within 24 to 48 hours.
- No: Adjust treatment strategy.
5. **Evaluate for Target Organ Damage**
- Is there evidence of target organ damage?
- Yes: Intensify treatment.
- No: Continue monitoring.
6. **Consider Special Conditions**
- Is the patient experiencing a pheochromocytoma crisis?
- Yes: Promptly lower BP but avoid aggressive reduction.
- No: Follow standard protocols.
7. **Assess for Sexual Dysfunction**
- Discuss potential impacts of antihypertensive medications on sexual function.
8. **End**
[END_FLOWCHART]
